Mr. ,1%-ln Burns recently said in a happy mixture of metaphors that the Imperial Institute was a white elephant, which the London Connty Council ought to take under its wing. The -remarlf has ap- parently stung t.-.e authorities at the Institute to a sense oi repartee, for on Monday afternoon (the Prince of Wales in the chair) Lord Herschel! ela- borately lectured to a small audience on the work of the Ins. itute. The Prince of Wales has held loyally by the Institute, which he practically creared,snd it is not his fault that it has not been a success. The truth is that from the first the social reputation of the place has been that it is the most snobbish haunt in London, given over to a class whose chief affection of it has been due to the Prince's fondness of his project. The general public take the faintest possible interest in it, and even the provincial sightseer prefers the collec- tions in the South Kensington Museum close by the Imperial Institute. It is a great pity to see so noble a building so constantly empty. Complaint has b en made by some ill-informed pers ,n that the Council have attempted to make the proceed- ings within its walls popular, hut that is exactly what they have not done. They have rather tried to make the proceedings as unattractive as pos- sible, with huge deserted rooms and sounding cor- dors as a result. Even as a club for the inhabi- tants of Kensington—and that is all it is at present —it leaves mach to be desired.

There has been ample time for King Prempeh's reply to the telegram of these worthies to '.)e received at Cape Coast Castle, but up to now his Sable majesty has made no sign, confirming the idea some rime ago ~xpressed in this column that be would use the telegram of his amhat.sadors as a pipplight. All the news at present to hand from the Gold Coast points to a defiant and fighting atti- tude on the [tart of the CoomasSie barbarian.

The occouchment of the Duchess of York was so unexpectedly early ti at it took by surprise even the Royal P.smilv. The Home Secretary's recent letter ex, using himself from attending a meeting at New- catche because of " unfortunate" necessity of hold- ing himself in readiness for R summons to Sandring- ham did something to awaken suspicion that the event Was not expected to be remote; but the general idea was that he was only showing the extreme caution of a man new to the office. But that the Princess's own family were caught napping is evident from the fact that not even the Duchess of leek could reach her. The expeditious character of the event contrasts pleasantly, both to the young mother herself and to the outer world, with the cirenmstances attending the birth of Prince Edward of York, ftt it will be remembered for how long a time tte nation waited for news from White Lodge, and how that residence was practically invested for days by a cordort of newspaper correspondents. Sorne of these had bicycles on which to race off to the nearest telegraph office, while one of surpassing enterprise had erecteA a tent in which, booted and spurred, he kept n horse ready saddled and bridled for a straight dash across country. There was none of this on Saturday morning, to the great relief, no doubt, of the inmates of York Cottage. Q iietness is indeed at all times characteristic of the York house- hold, and Court newsmen are sometimes inclined to throat up the task ef attempting to penetrate the Juv-tera of the un)stentatious movements of the Duke and Duchess Moreover, the dislike of their ftoyal 14 ighnessps to Society functions helps to keep riem a good deal out of sight. In this they reemhte tt e Duke' and Duchess of Fife, who are only too pleased to be let alone.

This sixteenth annual exhibition of " Truth's " home-made and other toys is an enormous affair. There are over 26 000 children in the London hospitals. workhouses, and infirmaries, and it is intended to give each of them a separate toy. Larder and more expensive toys—and of these there are abundance—are to he presented to the hospitals and workhouses for the general use of the inmates. A special feature of the show are the 3.500 dolls which have been dressed by the lady readers of "Truth " in connection with the competition for prizes for the best dressed dolls. Fifty are dre-sed by Mrs. Truefitt, who has now for many years gained the hidhest prize. Nothing can exceed the dainty and artistic manner in which she clothes and poses thit dolls. The home-made dolls show a dis- tinct advance this year both in quality and quan- tity, and most noteworthy amongst these being a gipsy caravan well laden with the usual dipsy waxes of Mass, twoomu. Szc. ; a fairy's bower; Ilansel and Oretel at the witch's cottage ; a show-room at a costumier's ; a scene in the studio from the "Artist's Model " a fancy dress ball, &c. Mrs. Cleveland, wife of President Cleveland, has kindly sent some dolls, and " Troth " has pent her some of the Bgltimnre Toy Show. The 11,000 sixpences have also again been presented by the anonymous donor.

So strong is the feeling in Liberal "circles at the continued inactivity of the Government on the Armenian clues ion that a movement is on foot amongst the unnfficia: members of the party to' hold a meeting of protest in the National Liberal Club, at which the Liberal leaders should be asked to attend. The Committee of the Club are to be appr,ach-tl, to allow a petition to be placed in the hali nit' e Club for signature. It is felt by many Liberals that this movement is all the more desir- able and necessary seeing that the projelted twitioral nna representative gathering in St.James'a flyer which ete Duke of Westminster was to liave p ea 'led, has been indefinitely postponed. The 1 lye of the Duke to abandon the meeting is bell., ve. to be tine to pressure brought to bear on him by Lord Salisbury, with whom he has been in communication for ten days. It has, I am told, been represented to him that a " pilgrimage of passion w,•tild he unwise at the present moment, and would hampar and impede the negotiations of the Foreign Om*. I understand that Mr. Kdward Atkin has tendered his resignation as secretary of the Anglo- Armenian Association to the Executive Committee, but this step has been taken on account of indiffer- ent health, and not, it is said,through disagreement with his colleagues.

THE CENTRE OF GRAVITY. - The following balancing trick will explain itself without any written description.

All that is necessary Is a stout visiting card, a small piece of wood, and a pocket-knife.

BrrE an inch off the poker. Remember it is "off" or away from the poker.

PLACE three chairs in a row, take off your boots, and jump over them. The boots, of course, being meant.

PLACE a piece cork in the mouth of a bottle and attempt to blow it into the bottle. The harder you blow the harder will the cork fly cn4

WHAT HAS NEVER BEEN SEEN BEFORE. —Pro- fess yourself able to show anyone what he never saw, what you never saw, what nobody else ever saw, and what, after you two have seen, nobody else ever shall see again. When the company have given up guessing what this wonderful thing can be, produce a nut, crack it, take out the kernel, and ask if they have ever seen that before. Of course they will answer "No." "Well," you say, "neither have I, and, I think, you will admit nobody else has ever seen it, and now no one shall ever see it again." Here you put the kernal in your mouth and eat it.

To REMOVE A BISCUIT FROM UNDER A PLATE. WITHOUT TOUCHING THE PLATE.—After some pre. liminary hankey-pankey, with which even Amateur conjurors are familiar, produce another biscuit (of the same kind as the one under the plate), and ask one of the company (a confederate by preference), to lift the plate and see if the biscuit is gone. When he lifts the plate, you remove the- biscuit, and, if the confederate knows his, business, he will replace the plate. You then make a flourish with the biscuit which was under the plate (haviiig meanwhile pocketed the other biscuit), and say, " You observe I have removed the biscuit from under the plate, without touching the plate."

To REMOVE THE MIDDLE PLATE FROM BETWEEN TWO O'FIIERS, WITHOUT TOUCHING IT.—Make a pile of three plates, and, after the usual patter, take off the top plate (being careful oot to touch the middle one), and place the other two on top of it.

To COVER A GLASS OF WINE WITH A TABLE- NAPKIN, AND TO CAUSE THE WINE TO DISAPPEAR, AS WILL BE SEEN WHEN THE TABLE-NAPKIN IS REMOVED.—Take the glass of wine in one hand, and with the other put the table-napkin over yom head, and let it also cover your hand and the glass, which are to be held up to your mouth. Drink the wine and then remove the table-napkin from off your head.

How does the Russian nation resemble the sea? —Because her nobles are tremendous swells and her people serfs. WHAT is the difference between a person late for the train and a school mistress ?—One misses the train and the other trains the misses.

ONE Of the fruits of civilisation is noticed in the fact that there are but a few varieties of cabbage, while the various kinds of cigars are innumerable.

TLIE TRIALS OF FRIENDSH—W.—Wife to husband in difficulties) : " Why don't you ask help from Griggs? He once said you could rely on him as long as he had a crust in the house." Husband (who knows Griggs) : "Yes, he meant my house."

GUEST : "What beautiful china ! I never saw anything like it before." Flower of Family: "Good l Ma wasn't sure whether yo t ever called on the Joneses or not."

CHRISTMAS CRACKERS.

CHARADES. My First is the opposite of night. My second is a weight. My whole is a city in Ohio. Dayton.

My first's a precious stone, My next a well-known tree; Or call my first a fruit, The next a thong will be. Whichever way you choose This puzzle to divide, You still will find my whole A powder will abide.

Pearl-ash.

My second sweepeth clean, 'tis said, When new; but housewives say That 'tis no good when constant use Hath worn its strefigth away. Ab, lazy son, your algebra You've very badly reckoned; My first shall point my whole for you In likeness of my second.

Birch Broom.

My whole's a word of letters five, I'm found both far and near; Behead me, and I am a sound That strikes upon the ear. My tail cut off, a weight now comes, Most useful to mankind; Belead again, my tail replace, A unit you will find. Curtail once more, and I am left A very little word; A preposition sometimes found, An adverb often heard. - Behead me now, my tail clap on, And then I think you'll find That I abound in man and beast, And also In mankind.

Stone. •

When o'er the western hills at close of day The sun is shedding a departing ray, He paints my first in glory on the skies In all the splendour of celestial dyes. My second, fitting emblem of the tomb, Pursues his sinuous way through paths of gloom Clothed in sad colours, yet at man's behest He causes man to be more richly drest. My whole, soft beacon of the summer night, Through darkness sends a beam of purest light; He who would find it need not gaze on high, Or search with curious eyes the starlit sky. Glow-warm.

Silence is golden, yet lam not gold, But rather a silvery hue have, I'm told;

I live but a month, yet I rapidly grow, And reflect in a manner thgt often I .throw

Upon subjects beneath me a beautiful light, And am steady, although often out late at night. As of all the things said of me, that is the wol•At, You surely can guess what I mean by my first.

Myssecond.is used in all buildings, I ween,

And likewise on steamboats,

in -aCtion,

rni seen.

The yachtsmen discourse of my breadth in a way. That is apt to lead dwellers on land quite astray. I'm found in the forest, I'm seen on the seas, And likewise am sought for inside of tall trees. My whole is a something transcendently light; Ijiide from the sun to appear in the night, . .

No chemist can weigh. ilia, I scoff at la scales. Now all try and guess me, and notice who fails. Moonbeam.

GAMES.

CATCRING TIIE MOUSE.—Nine players lay their hands one on the top of the other, saying "Ohe." 'Die lowest draws out his, puts it at the top, and cries "Two," and so on till "Nine" is cried. This last player seizes one of the hands which lie beneath his own (or more if he can), crying "I have caught the mouse," and.then the hand caught pays the forfeit; but it is not easy to catch one. At the word "Nine," all snatch away their hands as quickly as possible. The game must be played very fast to be funny. GAMB OF RUTH AND JACOB--Let the company form a circle, joining hands. Two of the company are placed in the centre of the circle as Ruth and Jacob, Jacob being blindfolded. It is the business (A Jacob to catch Ruth. For this purpose he must constantly cry our, "Where art thou, Ruth?" to which Ruth must answer immediately, " Here am, Jacob." As soon as Jacob has succeeded in catching Ruth, she must be blindfolded, and must choose some one from the surrounding ring to take the place of Jacob, whom she must pursue blind- folded until she has caught him. GRAND MUFTI is an old favourite. One player must be chosen to be Grand Mufti. He must stand. upon a chair in the full view of all, and make some gesture or grimace, saying with each, "So says the Grand Mufti" or "Thus says the Grand Mufti." When he used the word "so" the company must remain still ; but if he uses " thus " they must imitate him. A forfeit is imposed for each mistake. The cleverness of the Grand Mufti consists in throwing the players off their guard and causing them to make mistakes.

FIVE DOT GAux.—Any number can play; each must have paper and pencil. Let each player then make five dots on the paper in any position he pleases, and pass it on to the next player, who must draw some animal or human figure in such a position that one dot comes in the middle of the forehead, one at each hand, and one at the point of each foot. If the figure is an animal, one dot at each of the four feet. Only five minutes must be taken to draw the figure.

THE PAULI:JUR TOURNAMENT. —This is another game in which blindfolding is used, and is thus played :—Take a sheet of paper on which is in- scribed a circle, and tack to the wall about 4ift. from the floor. Let each boy (or "knight," as he should be denominated), as his name is called, take his position at the side of the room opposite the paper. Aftengetting his hand in position, so that he may use his index finger as a spear, he is blind- folded', and advances slowly; making his best effort to take the ring. The usual tournament programme may be carried out, and the successful knights allowed to crown a quee and maids of honour, and make a coronation speech, &c.

GAME OF MIND-READING.—A very puzzling form of the game of " mind-reading " is the following. Ask each of those who are to engage in the game to v rite upon a slip of paper the name of something. Put all these names in a box, and let the mind- reader draw from it the slips, one by one, pressing each slip against his forehead and, after pretended rumination, namin-, what is written. The first time he names the object he himself wrote, opens the paper as if for confirmation, and reads, of course, what not he, but some one else wrote. With the second slip he names this object, pro- ceeding thus until the last. Of course all in the room will agree that he has named precisely what they wrote, and it will be long before they can guess how the feat was accomplished. The reader must take care to select last of all the slip he him- self wrote, and the slips must be nearly enough alii<e, and folded in so uniform a way, that no one will recognise his own slip.

AszornEn Mrrn EXPLODLD.—Maiden Lady: "I have always heard that any one who sat under the mistletoe would be kissed." Niece: "Don't you believe it, auntie?" Maiden Lady: "No; I have sat under it all the evening, and I ain't had a smack yet."

A PLACE FOR. TIMM. —Mi.. Lovell: "Did you mean the silver hair-brushes for me?" Mrs. Lovell; " Yes : they're your Christmas present, dear." Mr. Lovell: "Thank you, Puss. That's just what I wanted ; but they're so elaborate and beautiful that I shall hardly dare to use them." Mrs. Lovell: "Oh, never mind. I thought we could put them in the guest room."

AN ALTERED CASE.—Mr. Brown: "Johnnie has been asking for a tin whistle and a drum." Brown: "Well, he can't have them." Mrs. Brown: "I forgot to tell you he was going to spend Christmas with mother." Brown: " out and buy them."

THE HIDDEN HAND; OR, CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. BY .111:3. N. D. E. N. SOUTHWORTH.

CHAPTER Xll7. THE WASTING HEART.

"Then she took -up the burden of life again, Saying only, 'lt might have been.' Alas for them both, and alas for us all, Who vainly the dreams of youth recall, For all sad words of lips, or pen, The saddest are these-1 It alight have been."'

-WHITTIER.

By the tacit consent of all parties, the meteor hope that had crossed and vanished from Marah Rocke's path of life was never mentioned again. Mother and son went about their separate tasks. Traverse worked at jobs all day, studied at night, and went twice a week to his patron, Doctor Day, at Willow Hill. Marah sewed as usual all day, and prepared her boy's meals at the proper times. But day by day her cheeks grew paler, her form thinner, her step fainter. Her son saw this decline with great alarm. Sometimes he found her in a deep, troubled reverie, from which she would awaken with heavy sighs. Sometimes he surprised her in tears. At such times he did not trouble her with questions that he instinctively felt she could not or would not answer; but he came gently to her aide, put his arms about her neck, stooped and laid her face against his breast, and whispered assurances of his "true love," and his boyish hopes of "getting on," of "making a fortune," and bringing "brighter days" for her ! And she would return his caresses, and with a faint smile reply that he "must not mind" her, that she was only "a little low-spirited," that she would "get over it soon." But as day followed day, she grew visibly thinner and weaker, dark shadows settled under her hollow eyes and in her sunken cheeks. One evening, while standing at the table washing up their little tea service, she +suddenly dropped into her chair and fainted. Nothing could exceed the alarm and dis- tress of poor Traverse. He hastened to fix her in an easy position, bathed her face and hands in vinegar and water—the only restoratives in their meagre stock—and called upon her by every loving epithet to live and speak to him. The fit yielded to his efforts, and presently, with a few fluttering inspirations, her breath returned and her eyes opened. Her very first words were attempts to re- assure her dismayed boy. But Traverse could no more be flattered. He entreated his mother to go at once to bed. And though the next morning, when she arose, she looked not worse than usual, Traverse left home with a heart full of trouble. But instead of turning down the street to go to his work in the town, he turned up the street towards the wooded hills beyond, now glowing in their gorgeous autumn foliage, and burning in the bril- liant morning sun. A half hour's walk brought him to a 'high and thickly wooded hill, up which a private road le4 through a thicket of trees to a handsome grey stone country seat, situated' in the midse of beautifully ornamented grounds, and known as Willow Heights, the residence of De. William Day, a retired physician of great repute, and a man of earnest piety. He was a widower with one fair daughter, Clara, a girl of fourteen, then absent at a boarding- school. Traverse had never seen this girl, but his one great admiration was the beautiful Willow Heights, and its worthy proprietor. He opened the highly ornate gate, and entered upon an avenue of willows that led up to the house, a two-storeyed edifice of grey stone, with full-length front piazzas above and below.

Arrived at the door, he rang the bell, which was answered promptly by a good-humoured looking boy, who at once showed Traverse to the library up stairs, where the good doctor sat at his books. Doctor Day was at this time about fifty years of age, tall and stoutly built, with a fine head and face, shaded by soft, bright flaxen hair and beard; thoughtful and kindly dark blue eyes, and an ear- nest, penetrating smile, that reached like sunshine the heart of any one upon whom it shone. He wore a cheerful looking flowered chintz dressing. gown corded around his waist; his feet were thrust into embroidered slippers; and he sat in his elbow- chair at his reading table, poring over a huge folio volume. The whole aspect of the man, and of his surroundings was kindly cheerfulness. The room opened upon the upper front piazza and the ,win- dows were all up to admit the bright morning sun and genial air, at the same time that there was a glowing fire in the grate to temper its chilliness. Traverse's soft step across the carpeted floor was not heard by the doctor, who was only made aware of his presence by his stepping between the sun- shine and his table. ' Then the doctor arose, and with his intense smile extended his hands, and greeted the boy with: • "Well, Traverse, lad, you are always welcome! I did not expect you until night, as usual, but as you are here, so much the better! Got your exercise all ready, eh ?—Heaven bless you, lad I what is the matter ?" inquired the good man suddenly, on first observing the boy's deeply troubled looks. "My mother, sir I my mother 1" was all that Traverse could at first utter.

"Your mother 1 My dear lad, what about her—is she ill ?" inquired the doctor, with interest. "Oh, sir, lam afraid she is going to die I" ex- claimed the boy in a choking voice, struggling hard to keep from betraying his manhood by bursting into tears.

"Going to die—oh pooh, pooh, pooh, she is not going to die, lad! tell me all about it," said the doctor, in an encouraging tone. "She has had so much grief, and care, and anxiety, sir—Doctor, is there any such malady as a broken heart ?" _ _ _

_ "Broken heart ?—pooh, pooh! no, my child, no never heard of such a thing in thirty years' medical experience ! Even that story of a porter who broke his heart trying to lift a ton of stone is all a fiction. No such disease as a broken heart. But tell me about your mother !" "It is of her that I am talking; she has had so much trouble in her life, and now I think she is sinkink under it; she has been failing for weeks, and last night, while washing the teacups, she fainted away from the table 1" "Heaven help us, that looks bad," said the doctor. "Oh, does it? does it, sir? She said it was no- thing much.' Oh, Doctor, don't say she will die 1 don't! if she were to die—if mother were to die, I'd give right up! I never should do a bit of good in the world, for she is all the motive I have in this life! To study hard—to work bard, and make her comfortable and happy, so as to make up to her for all she has suffered, is my greatest wish and en- deavour! Oh, don't say mother will die, it would ruin me 1" cried Traverse. "My dear boy, I don't say anything of the sort! I say, judging from your account, that her health must be attended to immediately. And—true I have retired from practice; but I will go and see your mother, Traverse !" "Oh, sir, if you only would! I came to ask you to do that very thing! I should not have presumed to ask such a favour for any cause but this of my dear mother's life and health, and you will go to see her?'

"Willingly and without delay, Traverse," said the good man, rising immediately and hurrying into an adjoining chamber. "Order the gig while I dress, Traverse, and I will take you back with me," he added, as he closed the chamber door behind him. _ .

By the time' Traverse had gone down, given the necessary orders and returned to the library, the doctor emerged from his chamber, buttoned up in his grey frock coat, and booted, gloved and capped for the ride. _ _

They went down together, entered the gig, and drove rapidly down the willow avenue, slow: y through the iron gate and through the dal thicket, and down the wooded hill to the high road, and then as fast as the sorrel mare could trot towards town. In fifteen minutes, the doctor pulled up his gig, at the right-hand side of the road before the cottage gate. They entered the cottage, Traverse going first in order to announce the doctor. They found Mrs. Rocke, as usual, seated in her low chair by the little fire, bending over her needle work. She looked up with surprise as they came in. "Mother, this is Doctor Day, come to see you," sald Traverse. She arose from her chair, and raised those soft and timid dark grey eyes to the stranger's face, where they met that sweet, intense smile that seemed to encourage while it shone upon her. " We have never met before, Mrs. Hooke, but we both feel too much interest in this good lad here to meet as strangers now," said the doctor, extending his hand. "Traverse gives me every day fresh cause to be grateful to you, sir, for kindness that we can never, never repay," said Marsh Rocke, pressing that bountiful hand, and then placing a chair, which the doctor took. Traverse seated himself at a little distance, and If thil cl9c_t_ oaversed vittakansl covertly examined

Ifia mother's fine, he watched the docto-r' countu- nance, as if life and death hung upon the character or its expression. But while they talked, not one word was said upon the subject of sickneqs or medicine. They talked of Traverse. The doctor assured his mother that her son was a boy of such fine talent, character and promise, that he had already made such rapid progress in his clmsical and mathematical studies, that he ought immedi- ately to enter upon a course of reading for one of the learned professions. The mother turned a smile full of love, pride and sorrow upon the fine, intellectual face of her boy, and said:

"You are like the angel in Cole's picture of life. You point the youth to the far-up temple of fame--"

"And leave him to get there as he can. Not at all madam I Let us see. Traverse, you are now going on eighteen years of age; if you had your choice, which of the learned professions would you prefer for yourself—law, physic, or divinity 2" The boy looked up and smiled, then dropped his head and seemed to reflect.

"Perhaps you have never thought upon the sub- ject. Well, you must take time—you must take time! so as to he firm in your decision when you have once decided," said the doctor. " Oh, sir, I ha ‘e thought of it long! and my choice has been long and firmly decided, were I only free to follow it 1" _ _

"Speak lad I What is your choice '1" ' "Why, don't you know, sir Can't you guess? Why, your own profession, of course, sir! Certainly, sir, I coutd not think of any other l" exclaimed the boy, with sparkling eyes and flushed cheeks. "That's my own lad I" exclaimed the doctor, enthusiastically seizing the boy's hand with one of his, and clapping the other down upon his palm; for if the doctor had an admiration in the world it was for his own profession. "That's my own, lad! My profession the healing art! why, it is the only profession worthy the study of an immortal being. Law sets people by the ears together I Divinity should never be considered as a profession—it is a divine mission! Physic! physic, my boy! The healing art I that's the pro- fession for you! And I am very glad to hear you declare for it, too; for now the way is perfectly clear 1"

Both mother and son looked up in surprise. "Yes, the way is perfectly clear. Nothing is easier! Traverse shall come and read medicine in my office. I shall be glad to have the lad there. It will amuse me to give him instruction occasion- ally! I have a positive mania for teaching." "And for doing good! Oh, sir, how have we deserved this kindness at your hands? and how shall we ever, ever repay it ? " cried Mrs. Rocke, In a broken voice, while the tears filled her gentle eyes. "Oh, pooh, pooh a mere nothing, ma'am! a mere nothing for me to do, whatever it may prove to him. It is very hard, indeed, if I am to be crushed under a cart load of thanks for doing some- thing for a boy I like, when it does not cost me a cent of money, or a breath of effort." "On, sir, your generous refusal of our thanks does but deepen our obligation," said Marah, still weeping. "Now, my dear madam, will you persist in making me confess that it is all selfishness on my part? I like the boy, I tell you! I shall like his bright, cheerful face in my office. I can make him very useful to me, also--" "Oh, sir! if you can and wi// only make him use- ful to you—" "Why, to be sure I can, and will! He can act as my clerk, keep my accounts, write my letters, drive out with me, and sit in the gig while I go in to visit my patients, for though I have pretty much retired from practice, still--" "Still you visit and pre ,cribe for the sick poor, gratis!" added Marah, feelingly. "Pooh, pooh! habit, madam, habit! 'ruling passion strong in death,' etc. I can't, for the life of me, keep from giving people bread pills! And now, by the way, I must be off to see some of my patients In Staunton I Traverse, my lad—my young medical assistant, I mean—are you willing to go with me? " "Oh, sir," said the boy, and here his voice broke down with emotion.

"Come along, then 1 " laughed the doctor; "you shall drive with me into the village as a commence- ment."

Traverse got his hat, while the doctor held out his hand to Mrs. Rocke, who, with her eyes full of tears, and her voice faltering with emotion, began again to thank him, when he good-humouredly interrupted her by saying: "Now, my good little woman, do—pray—hush! I'm a selfish fellow, as you'll see Ido nothing but what pleases my own self, and makes me happy! Good-bye! God bless you, madam!" he cried, cordially shaking her band. "Come, Traverse," he added, hurriedly striding out of the door and through the yard, to the gate before which the old green gig and sorrel mare were still waiting. "Traverse, I brought you out again to-day, more especially to speak of your mother and her state of health," said Doctor Day, very seriously, as they both took their seats in the gig and drove on towards the town. "Traverse, your mother is in no Immediate danger of death, in fact, she has no disease whatever!"

"Oh, sir, you do not think her ill, then I thought you did not, from the tact that you never felt her pulse, or gave her a prescription ex- claimed Traverse, delightedly, for in one thing the lad resembled his mother—he was sensitive and excitable—easily depressed and easily exhilarated. "Traverse, I said your mother is in no immediate danger of death, for that in fact she has no disease; but yet, Traverse, brace yourself up, for I am about to strike you a heavy blow! Traverse! Marah Rocke Is—starving !" "STARVING! Heaven of Heavens! no! that is not so I it cannot be! My mother starving! oh, horrible! horrible! But, Doctor, it cannot—cannot be! Why, we have two meals a day at our house! " cried the boy, almost beside himself with agitation. "Lad, there are other starvations beside the total lack of food there are slow starvat ions and divers ones! Marah Hooke is starving slowly and in every way mind, soul and body I her body is slowly wasting from the want of proper nutriment, her heart from the want of human sympathy, her mind from the need of social intercourse. Her whole manner of life must be chauged if she is to live at all!"

"Oh, sir, I understand you now! I feel, I feel that you speak the very truth Something must be done! I must do something. What shall it be? Oh, advise me, sir!" "I must reflect a little, Traverse," said the doctor, thoughtfully, as he drove along with very slack reins._

"And, oh, how thoughtless of me! I forgot, Indeed I did, sir, when I so gladly accepted your offer for me to read with you, I forgot thgt if I spent every day reading in your office, my mother would sadly miss the dollar and a half a week I made by doing little odd jobs in town." "But / did not forget it, boy; rest easy upon that score; and now let me reflect how we can best serve your good little mother I" said the doctor, and he drove slowly and thoughtfully along for about twenty minutes before he spoke again, when he saki:

"Oh, Doctor Day, if I could only thank you aright!" cried Traverse. " Pooh, pooh I nonsense! there is no time for it. Here we are at Spicer's grocery store, where I sup- pose you are again employed. Yes? Well, jump out then. You can still make half a day. Mind, remember on Monday next, December Ist, you enter my office as my medical student, and by that time I shall have some plan arranged for your mother. Good-bye God bless you lad !" said the good doctor, as he drove off and left Traverse standing in the genial autumn sunshine, with his heart swelling and his eyes overflowing with excess of gratitude and happiness.

TOR ENAMINVH. SATUR I)AY, DECEMBER 21, 1895.

the wagons putting into the yard on the new road, they must not have them stopping on the cart road. He was aware of telling cooper that he had to give them a b-- humpy, and Ire did not tell him that he must give them leather. He said they should want the a agora in a little sharper than they had put them in before, so as to enable them te go round the curve in the yard. He wanted thy wagons to stop between the curve and the machine. He gave the signal for them to stop before they got off the main line. He saw the wagons coming !in very sharp, and he tried to put the brakes down. He missed the first one, but ,tot the second, and after poll- ing it down he made for the last brake and stuck to that until the first wagon crashed through the doars, when he jumped off. He should think the wagons were tra- veliing at 10 m;les an hour. There were two other men on the first and second wagons, and he An d Carter were on the brake of the fourth wagon, there was nobody on the brake of the third wagon. There were four men on the brakes. From where the new line joins the old, right up to the gates was 64 yards it was a little down hill. The wagons travelled about 50 or 60 yards after they crashed through the doors. He had never seen wagons shunted into the yard at this speed before. He did nothing nor said anything to cause them to send the wagons in at an unusual speed. By Mr.Daviea : The last witness said you kept waving him on with your hand,aud after he had stopped the en- gine you kept waving on, is that true ? No, sir. Idi not wave the engine on after the wagons got through the g..tes off the main line. By Mr. Blind : What is the meaning of the word bumper ? I don't know.—As a Lancashire man, don't , you know what it means P—l had never heard the word until Mr. Glover mentioned it an Friday in the paint shop.—ls it your duty after getting wagons into he Vulcan Works to sects the pinning of the brakes ? Yes. He had three other men assisting on that date.— Why did you want four brakesmen to put down brakes on this particular occasion ? One was holding the points, f was seeing to the brakes,—Then yoh were expecting wagons to be sent in pretty smartly, when you had three or four men to assist you ? No.—Then why did ' you want so many men ? To assist if anything went wrong.—You say you gave a signal just before entering the yard,who did you give it to ? The engine driver or Dtn,the shunter,whichever could see m..—Was it to the driver or the shnnter you gave the signal P—l was just calling on him.—Who ? The driver.—Then you were signalling direct to the driver to bring the wagons into the yard ? Yes.—Did you notice what the driver did before you turned round to enter the yard ? No. _4a_ the The Coroner : D) you mean to say 1.1 h d dis- obeyed your instructions ? Well, he kept coming on. . .

Mr. Bland You .ay you gave the driver instrUctions to stop, and that you previously gave him instructions, but took no notice of them ? Yes.—And vet you turned your back on him,and went into the yard ? I went into the yard.

By the Coroner: The driver had shut off steam when he turned to go into the yard. By Mr. Bland : Why did you tell the shunter you wanted them put in sharply ? To get them round the curve.—Bad the wagons to be put in the shed where the doors were? No, they had to go to some other place.— Is it not a fact that wagons were shunted on this siding and not far enough for your convenience,and you had to use horses to take them further ? We have had tcruse horses. The rails were slippery, and the wind would be blowing over the railway towards the works, and with the incline would tend to make the wagons run quicker. . . _

The Coroner : Do you say that the London and North- Western Company have no responsibility over these wagons after they leave the Company's main line ? No. —Then who takes the responsibility ? I do.—Then who takes the responsibility of the speed to be shunted into the yard ? The Company's shunter.—But if be is not aware of the destination of the wagons how can he form any opinion as to the speed to be put upon them ? I don't know.—You know where you want them ? Yes. Then you are in a better position to form an opinion as to the speed they have to go in than the shunter of the Dmapany ? Well we want them to go into the yard, that's all. . .

_ By the foreman : Cooper did not ask him where the wagons had to go. Two of the wagon brakes were de- fective, but if they had been in proper order he did not think the accident could have been prevented. Joseph Clare, 352 Crow-lane, Newton, engine driver in the employ of the London and North-Western Company corroborated the first part of the evidence of Cooper. He said the four wagons ran at a fair pace. He did not know that anything hnd happened until about half-past two. He received his signals from Cooper,who told him that the Vulcan people had informed him that they wanted them well inside the vard,and that he must hit them up. He saw Sankey but did not take any notice of him. If he gave him any signals he would not take them. When the wagons left him,they would be travel- ling at the rate of five or six miles an hour. He took no responsibility but simply worked to the instructions of his guard. If they did not give the wagons a smart push they weuld not go round the curve on a dry day. John Baxter, Chapel-lane, Burtonwood, guard in the empley of the London and North-Western Company said beheld the points while the engine was drawing the nine empty wagons out. Sankey told them that he wanted to have the wagons on the new line in the vard,and they had to give them some b— humpy. The accident which occurred at the Vulcan Foundry, Newton le-Willows, on Thursday the 12th inst., to Her- man S. Williams, a fitter employed at the works, resulted fatally on Sunday morning, the deceased never having recovered consciousness. WARRINGTON SAVINGS BANK.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the trustees and managers of the above bank was hold at the Bank premises, Butter- market-st., on Tuesday, Major Pickmere, J.P., in the. chair. There were also present—The Rector (the Rev Canon Willis), the Revs. H. V. Pigot, G. B. Thurston, John Yonge, and J. J. Laugh= ; and Messrs. William Long, J.P. (vice-chairman), Robert Garnett, J.P., William Tinsley, John Harrison, F. Monks, J.P., C.C., Alderman Harrison, J.P., John Peers, D. W. Finney, C. Jackson Holmes, E. L. Fleming, Alderman R. W. Francomb, J.P., John Geddes, J.P., Alderman Edward Greenall, J.P., J. H. Fleming, J. 1., and John E. Davies (actuary).

ANIMAL REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE OF MANAGEMENT.

The folio% Mg report of the Committee of Management was read by the Actuary and apr roved The Com- mittee of Management present the annexed general statement of the Bank for the year ending 20th Novem- ber, 1895. As shown in the balance sheet the total assets are £311,874 114 61., the total amount due to depositors is £307,236 3s. 9d., showing a sur- plus of £4,638 7s. 9.1. Beyond the £308,859 3s. 10d. the Government have to the credit of the Bank, they hold £9,742 9s. Bd. 21 per cent, consolidated stock on account of those of our depositors who have invested in stock. The business done by the Bank during the past year must be considered very satisfactory-18,771 transaction in tbe year having taken place, the largest number the Bank has ever made ; and 730 new accounts have been opened in the year, resulting in an increase of 221 in accounts, and the to:al number of our depositors now is 11,126. £7,231 12s. 3d. interest has been credited the dery eit”rs' accounts, and the amount due to them is £307,236 3s. 91., and increase in amount of £11;594 18s. lu. for the year. The Government Inspectors have during the year attended at the Bank, and we are pleased to state that the business is conducted properly under the pro- ' visions of the Acts of Parliament. The extracted list of depositors' balances, certified by the auditor, is placed on the Bank counter during the hours of business, and any depceitor may personally satisfy himself that the amount in his pass book is certified to the Government as being to his credit. A copy of the Government certificate is given with this, certifying an amount being with them much larger than the amount we are due to our deposi- tors, and the original certificate may be seen at the Bank by any depositor desiring to see it. Being able to present so favourable a report is some encouragement to us in superintending the working of this provident institution, and seeing that the depesitors are properly secured for the amount lodged with us. The facilities offered, we think, are ample. The Bank is opened daily for receiving or paying—sums as low as a shilling are received. Fifty pounds mil be deposited in one year, and £2OO in the whole : £2OO may be received in one year for investment in G .vernment stock; payments are made on demand up to £lO, and any larger amount at a week's notice. Ths increased limit of deposit is highly appreciated, and we trust careful people in our district will continue to see the advantage of making use of this old established Savings Bank." The report was signed ley Major Picksnere and Mr. William Long. The CHAIRMAN then matted "That the statement of accounts prepared by the Actuary and examined by the auditor, is approved,and copies are directed to be trans- mitted to the National Debt Commissioners and to the Committee of Inspection, and that the duplicate state- ment be publicly affixed in the Savings Bank." In doing so he said that he was delighted to inform them that at last they had got over £300,000 due to the depositors. For some years the trustees bad been wishing to reach this amount, and it was some gratification to be able to report such a large amount being deposited by the pro- vident and sensible people in the town and neighbour- hood. When their Actuary was appointed the amount was just over £200,000, and he was sure he was pleased at the growth of the Bank by £lOO,OOO during his con- trol of the business. Another satisfactory feature was the large increase in the number of depositors. In the same time that they had increased in amount by £loo,ooo,they had doubled the number of their accounts. There were now 10,799 individual depositors, 139 triendly societies, 178 charitable societies, and ten penny banks using the Bank, totalling 11,126 accounts. The Government held to their credit a much larger amount than tbrey owed the depositors and the safety of their money lodged was be- yond question. Parents should encourage their children in provident habits, 'and he knew of no better way than opening an account for them with their Bank, and per- suading them to deposit their savings. The systematic habit of saving was the best.To those who determinedly come weekly,or monthly,the result was always satisfac- tory to themselves. (Applause.) The past has been one of the most prosperous years he had bad. His great desire had been that the amount deposited should reach £300,000. That had been done, and it spoke well for a population of from 50,000 to 60,000 thap such a large sum of money should be deposited in the Savings Bank. He hoped they would continue to increa.se,and that they would have an institution with which they were de- lighted, and which was a real benefit to the town and neighbourhood. (Applause.) Mr. WILLI= LONG seconded the motion, which was supported by .Mr. J. H. FLEMING, and heartily agreed to.

===== 0002601_18951221_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS TO THE COMMITTEE.

The Rev. H. V. PIGOT moved" That the report of the Committee of Management is approved, and that this meeting acknowledges the valuable services rendered to the institution by the gentlemen acting on the commit- tee during the past year. and tenders its best thanks to I them." Continuing, he remarked that the statement of the accounts was eminently satisfactory, and the report of the committee had given them all much pleasure. The bank was conducted on a perfectly sound basis, and it was gratifying to know that instead of losing ground they were increasing every year. There were indica- , dons of prosperity to Warrington, and the present was ' the first year for the last ten years in which the amount deposited had exceeded the amount withdrawn. That was a hopeful feature, and showed that they had begun to have brighter times in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Mr. E. L. frmanio seconded the motion, and said it would be gratifying if they could get a little more in- terest for their money. He supposed there was no pos- sible chance of investing their money except in Govern- ment securities. If there was a chance of investing in municipal securi lies the public of Warrington would get more for their money.

The motion was agreed to, and Mr. LoNo, in acknowledging the compliment on behalf of the Committee of Management, said that it was a great encouragement to them to know that their services were appreciated. The work of the Bank was carried on con amore, and with a single eye to the benefit of the people generally. There was one sequel to the remarks of the Chairman, which spoke very well for the present position of Warrington,and for the thriftiness of a large portion of the inhabitants. The increase in the War- rington Savings Bank did not appear to be a large amount. In 1885 they had £290,000 deposited, now the amount was £17,000 more. But during the last 20 years it should be remembered that the Post Office Sav- ings Bank had been in existence. In that Bank, it appeared that the depositors numbered 5,744, and the amount of looney deposited was £57,500, so that they would see Warrington had progressed much mere quickly with regard to its habits of thriftiness than the single report of the Savings Bank, in Buttermark-st. showed. He could only say in responding to the vote of thanks that it was not the Committee of Management which deserved their thanks alone,but they were well and ably supported by the Actuary and the other officers of the Bank. (Hear, hear.) Much credit was no doubt due to them for its successful management. (Applause.)

SIMOESTED REMOVAL OF THE RANK PREMISES.

Mr. F. Mosacs moved "That the following managers be elected to join the trustees on the Committee of Management for the ensuing year :—The Revs. John Yonge and W. H. Drummond, Messrs. John Peers, J. H. Fleming, Thomas Whittle, John Harrison, Charles Jack- son Holmes, and Henry Roberts." Mr. Monks said there was one matter to which he wished to refer, and about which he would like an expression of opinion, and that was as to the desirability of moving the Bank business. They had a reserve fund of from £4,000 to £5,000, which could not be used unless for some useful purpose in connection with the Bank. The present build- ing was very uncomfortable. The room in which they were then assembled was a very poor one for a com- mittee to meet in. It was cold, and uncomfortable in every way, and he did not believe in spending any more money on the present premises. They had spent quite enough to have built a new bank in the centre ot the town. The present position, whatever it might have been in 1822, was not a very central position to-day,and he believed that there were premises in the centre of the town which, if acquired, would add very much to the facilities of business as well as to the comfort of the officers. He should be pleased to have an expression of opinion on the matter, as he be'ieved they should pos- sass premises which would meet their future require- ments. Whatever else might be said about Post-office Savings Banks, it would be admitted that there was such a good deal of redtapeism about the Government and all its institutions that he believed their own Savings Bank was supplying a long-felt want. (Hear, hear.) Mr. D. W. FINNEY seconded the motion which was agreed to.

A JAPANESE COMMERCIAL INVASION OF THE UNITED STATES.

Wasainovon. MONDAY.—In the Senate to-day Mr. Stewart, speaking on the subject of a pending resolution for the appointment of the commission to investigate the needs of labour and agriculture, predicted an inva- sion of Oriental products. lie valid that Japanese agents were now in the United States offering bicycles,matches, door and window sashes, cooperage, hats, gloves, and other wearing apparel at 50 per cent.below the prices of the same articles made in the United States.

THE SHIPBUILDERS' DISPUTE.

On Saturday a ballot was taken by the Glasgow and Clyde Bank eagineers with reference to the terms offered by the masters at the recent Conference. Only 112 of the Glasgow men voted for the acceptance of the terms, 1,557 voting in favour of their rpjec ion. The Clyd frank men rejected the offer by 315 votes to 10. There is, therefore, little prospect of the dispute between the shipbuilders on the Clyde and at Belfast and their en- gineers being settled before January. Lord James, the Lord Provost of Glasgow, and the Lord Mayor of Belfast are'of opinion that no good is likely to follow the callins, together of the members of the conference which recently met in Glasgow. Great distress prevails both in Glasgow and Belfast.

===== 0002601_18951221_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
EUROPEAN NAVIES COMPARED.

In a speecla delivered a few months ago, the Italian Minister of Marine made a statement to the effect that the naval expenditure of Italy was laid out with much better discernment than that of other Powers. England spent five times as much money, yet her naval power was only three and a half times that of Italy ; France spent three times the money of Italy, and had a naval power only equal to twice that of Italy ; Russia's naval strength was about equal to that of Italy, with double the expenditure ; and Germany spent the same as Italy, and had an inferior naval force.

It is interesting to compare the above with the follow- ing, culled from a newspaper : " in naval circles, much interest has been aroused by a discussion as to the relative cost of British and foreign ironclade, and though calculations made by German ex- perts differ to some extent frem those made at White- hall, it is agreed, first, that we build more cheaply than foreigners,and, secondly,that the cost of construction at our Government dockyards has diminished. Mr. White asserts that, while the Royal Sovereign class of 14,000 tons cost on an average £775,000, the United States lowa, of 11,000 tons cost £900,000, while France pays from £900,000 to a million for ea first-class battleship,and Italy and Russia each a million. Taking typical ironclads, German authorities give the British expenditure at £6O per ton. American at £7B, and Ger- man and French at from £B7 to £9O. Then, comparing the present with the past, twenty years ego the In- flexible, which displaces 2,300 tons less than the Royal Sovereign, is slower by four knots an hour, and less powerful, altogether cost £812,000, or £40,000 than the newer ironclad. In all these cases, speed of construc- tion is considered an important factor in producing the difference in cost."

===== 0002601_18951221_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
CHRISTMAS.

The longest night of all the year, And gloomy shortest day ; On the eve of Merry Christmas, Again have passed away. Gladly we rest a HUI, while From every toil and care, And for the welcome holiday, We joyfully prepare. Forgiving all unkindness now, That grieved us in the past ; Without a thought of enmity, For love will come at last. With homage still we think of Him, The Nazirine who bore Through death the saving love divine, Though crown of thorns he wore. Who told us of a better state, From all our vices free ; Where sympathy and truthfulness, Shall reign in purity. Throughout all time His spotless life, By men revered will be ; Who brought us hopes of peace and love, And immortality.

Warrington, Dec. 1895.

S. KENYON,

PENKETH.

GREAT SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.—A meeting ot the Great Sankey Parish Council took place on Mon- day evening in the Board Schools, Great Sankey, Mr. W. P. Skelton presided, and there were present—Messrs. H. Bate (vice chairman), George Thompson, T. Halton. J. 1 Pairclough, W. H. L. Gorse, J.Kilshaw, and J. Wright (clerk and assistant overseer). The Chairman produced a statement showing how the fund of RI forming the Parish dole, had been distributed. It was the only fund continued the chairman, which the Council had the pleasure of distributing, and the allowances had been paid according to toe conditions, which stipulated that only those widows who resided in the parish, and who had received relief within the past twelve months,should be entitled to the money.—Mr. Thompson asked if any thing had been done with reference to Barnes' Bridge ? —Mr. Bate replied that be had heard nothing from Mr. John Thompson, the Surveyor to the Warrington Dis- trict Highway Board, to whom he had mentioned the matter on more than one occasion. He did not think the Highway Board was the responsible body ; it would rather more likely be for the County Council to deal with.—The Chairman thought a. recommendation from the Highway Board or of the Parish Council was neede.i. The bridge certainly wanted improving very badly. The side of the bridge abutted too much upon the footpath, and was very dangerous to pedestrians. If he were to walk against it, and were to injure himself, he should certainly enter an action for damages either against the Highway Board, the County Council, or whoever was the responsible authority. He suggested that it be a recom- mendation to be Clerk to write to the Highway Commit- tee of the County Council calling attention to the dangerous condition of the bridge, and the desirability lof having it remedied as early as possible.—Mr. Thompson proposed, and Mr. Halton seconded, a resolution to this effect, and it was carried.— Mr. Halton said there was another bridge he would like to call the attentiota of the Council to, and that was the one leading to Whittle's farm. It was in a very dan- gerous condition.—The Chairman thought it was a pri- vate bridge, and, therefore, not a matter for their inter- ference.—Mr. Halton said it was a public footpath, and it should be attended to. The bridge was more dangerous than Barnes' Bridge. It wanted building up at the sides. —The Chairman suggested that the attention of Lord Lilford's agent should be drawn to the dangerous con- dition of the bridge, although he had always been under the impression that it was a private road, and that con- sequently it. was not their business t. interfere. This course was adopted, on the motion of Mr. Halton, se- conded by Mr. Bate.—On the proposition of Mr.llalton, seconded by Mr. Thompson, it was decided to lurchese a tin deed box for the purpose of storing the Clerk's papers and books, the cost of such box not to exceed £1 lg.—Mr. Thompson gave notice that at the next meeting of the Council he intended to move that the Council should try to dispose of the parish chest which they had recently gained possession of. It would fetch much more than would be required to pay for the tin box, and it was serving no practical purpose now.—The meeting then Closed.

LYMII.

PRIMROSE LEAGUE MEETING.—On Monday evening a soire- was held in connection with the above League in the Assembly Room, Conservative Club, Lymm. The chair was occupied by Mrs. U. Greenall, ruling coun- cillor. There were also on the plitforrn—Mrs.Callender, Mrs. N ,rris, Mrs. E. Crosfieid, and Mr. Henry Ackerley, K.C.P.L. After speeches by Mrs. Greenall and Mr. Ackerley, Mr. T. P. Norris proposed a vote of thanks to Mr.Ackerley, and spoke in high terms ot his work during the recent election. This was s,conded by Mr. F. Swallow, and heartily agreed to. A vote of thanks was also accorded to Mrs. Greenall, on the motion of Mr. A. L. Callender. A musical programme was then gone through by Mis Kate Sinclair and Mr. W. R. Duncan, songs, duets, pianoforte solos, and humorous selections being rtmdered in excellent style. Ft-Se artistes con- cluded their programme with an amusing dramatic dia- logue, entitled " Sympathy," much mirth being caused during the performance. The remaining portion of the evening was devoted to dancing, which was kept up with much spirit until an early hour on the following morning.

GIFT FOR THE DAdESBIIRY POOR.

The usual parcel of neediework !IBS just been received at the Vicara,e, from the West Cheshire Needle Guild, through Mrs. Lyon and Miss Tinley. The articles contained therein will be cbstributed smongst the deserving poor of the parish by Mrs. Whitley, THE DARESDURY SCHOOL ENDOWMENT FUND. _ _

A meeting ot the governors of aireshury School Endowment Fund was held at the Vicarage on. Tuesday, when there were present—Mr. Philip Derbyshire (chairman), the Rev. T. Whitley. Messrs. Henry Mawdaley, John Cooper, and R. Earp, C.C., The statements ofeccounts already paid were read and considered. The distribution of the money prizes to the schools at Daresbury, Preston Brook, Moore and Hatton was next discussed, and it was ultimately decided to make the distribution at Daresbury School on Monday, the 6th January, at Hatton School on Tuesday afternoon, at Moore School on IVeinesday afternoon. and at Preston Brook School on Thursday afternoon. It was also unanimou,ly decided that this year the prizes at the infant schools will be given in money, and not in clothing as heretofore. The meeting then ter- minated. BRICK STREET MISSION BAND OF HOPE SOCIETY. The young women in connection with the above society did tl ernselves credit in the catering for the third entertainment given in the schoolroom on Tuesday, the various items of the programme being very creditably rendered; There was a large atten- dance, presided over by Mrs. Councillor Henry Roberts whose choice and feeling address was listened to with close attention. The following re- cited; Masters James Wrench,Thomas Hill, Samuel Gallop, Misses Lily Looker, Elizabeth Gallop, Bessie Shaw, Elizabeth Jackson, and Annie Heywood. Soloist, Master Isaac Shaw. Two choruses by the juvenile choir were sung with pleasing effect. Duets entitled " Sweet Music," and " Home Sweet Home,' were rendered by Misses Annie Warburton and Mary Ellen Gallop. The morals of the dialogues "The Children's Cnoice,' "Be true to the Pledge," and " The Outcome of Firmness," were very clear and pointed, the re-pec- tive characters being ably sustained by those taking part. A hearty vote of thanks was given to Mrs. Roberts for presiding, to which she suitably tea- ponder]. A pleasant evening entertainment termin- ated with the,minging of the doxology.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, December 21st, 1895.

• TEE political sensation of the week Politica Se has come from a very unexpected nsation. quarter. It does not consist, as might have been naturally anticipated, in any news from the Near East or the Far East, but is contained in a message which President CLEVE- LAND hash sent to the United States Congress. Looked at from one point of view it would be im- possible to exaggerate the gravity of the message so far as the future relations of Great Britain and the United States—the two great branches of the English-speaking race—are concerned. Fortu- nately, there are many reasons for believing that the situation is not really so serious as it appears to be on the face of it. The points in the President's Message to which we allude are, it is needless, to say his references to the boundary dispute be- tween the South American colony of British Guiana and the Spanish, American Republic of ' Venezuela. Acting on the strength of what is 11 known as the "Monroe doctrine " the Government of the United States has asked that the British Government should agree to submit the claims of the Venezuelans to arbitration. To this request Lord SALISBURY has, in effect, replied that this country has no objection to arbi- tration in regard to the greater portion of the dis- puted territory; but, he continues- "they cannot consent to entertain or to submit to the arbitra- tion of another Power, or of foreign jurists how- ever eminent, claims based on the extravagant pretensions of Spanish officials in the last century, and involving ;the transfer of large numbers of British subjects, who have for many years enjoyed the settled rule of a British colony,to a nation of a different race and langlage, whose political system is subject to frequent disturbance,and whose insti- tutions as yet too often afford very inadequate protection for life and property." It has already been made clear in the Press and elsewhere that in making this last reservation Lord SALISBURY has the fullest support of Englishmen of every party. It may be taken for granted that this country is not going to withdraw its protec- tion from its own colonists simply because the hybrid commonwealth of Venezuela choses to make a claim for an extension of territory. Yet because Lord SALISBURY has made this reserva- tion, President CLEVELAND has sent a message to the American Congress which contains" a direct threat o war against this country. He suggests that Congress should appoint a commission to inquire into l.the merits of the dispute, and if it reports against the claims of Great Britain, and he seems to take it for granted that it will, he advises that the United States should" resist by every means in its power, as, a wilful aggression upon its rights and interests,the ' appropriation by Great Britain of any lands,or the I exercise of governmental jurisdiction over any territory which, after investig.tion,we have deter-

===== 0002601_18951221_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER SATUR'A Y, DECEMBER 21 1895.

mined of right to belong to Vonezuela."Th is it will be seen is a monstrous and previonsly unheard of proposition. It amounts to this, that in the event of any dispute between Great Britian or presumably any other European power, and one of the petty and turbulent South American Re- publics, the United States is to decide, whether invited or uninvited, which party is in the right and to impose its decision on the two disputants. President CLEVELAND, and those who support him,will soon begin to see that the application of the Monroe doctrine which is now proposed is so extraorditary that it will not be acquiesced in during this generation at all events. The United States is no doubt a great country with a great future, but it is not yet a giant amongst pygmies. In setting up as the final arbiter in all ' disputes affecting the whole continent of America, it is attempting more than it can perform. State-Secretary OLNEY,in his correspondence with the British Foreign Office, assumes in a rather ,J cool,not to say insolent,way that the States is the only great power represented in America, and the reminder by Lord SALISBURY that a connection still exists between Great Britain and such thriv- ing communities as Canada, Jamaica, Honduras, and other colonies, was not out of place. The "New York World" is even more to the point when,in condemning what it calls the PRESIDENT'S " jingo bugaboo," it points to the fact that this country controls more territory even in America than is contained in the United Stet

A. VERY attractive picture of A l'eroolgorneyss. v e social life in that most progressive of all British colonies, New Zea- land, is given by • Mr. EDWARD REE VES in this mouth's "Westminster Review." The *Land Laws there are, according to the testimony of an American Consul, superior to those of any other country in regard to the check that they place upon the acquisition of vast estates by single persons. The laws have been devised with a view to the discouragement of private monopolies of any kind, and the result is that wealth is much more equally diffused than in most other coun- tries. There are, it is said, no millions, ires in the colony, and, oa the other hand, there are no paupers. The "old-world terror of abso- lute penury is unknown" to the New Zea- lander. If a man be loft friendless and without employment he can turn for assist- ' awe ta the Labour Bureau. "If there be no room for him in any trade or job, he goes "says Mr. REEVES," on the land, to the Rauri gumfield, to the 'bush section' which Government will par- tially clear for him, by the State saw-mill of the almost inexhaustible forest. He cannot starve." There is something taking about the following picture or the school life of New Zealand children. "He is not left to books and his inner conscious- ness to form ideas of a forest or a factory. He is taken to see them. Free periodical excursions of whale schools by railway are organised. Country children come to town, where they are re- ceived by school committees, who conduct them over museums, newspaper °frees, gas works, ocean steamers, and explain everything A thousand town children see a field of yellow wheat eaped and bound, write essays on the matter, and ever after distinguish the grain from barley or oats. Scholarships are for the poorest labourer's sons if he be clever ; techni- cal workshops, if he be of a technical turn; state I farms if he lean to agriculture." The adult work- man does not seem to have much to complain of. This working day is one of eight hours; and he is protected in various ways by enlightened labour legislation, and, in fact, taking everything into account ,there seems to be good grounds for the opinion that ha has more opportunities ,for "rest, recreation, and intellectual development than is enjoyed by his fellow-workers in any part of the world."

TREAT TO POOR CIIILDREN.

On Christmas Day, the Rev Dr. Gastaldi has arranged to give a hot-pot dinner to 300 or 400 poor children,irre- soeotive of creed, in St. Edward's school. Further par- ticulars will he obtained from our advertising columms. WESLEYAN DORCAS SOCIETY, RUNCORN. . .

On Tuesday afternoon in connection with the St. Paul's Dorcas Society, a distribution of garments was made. About forty flannel garments and a number of other useful wearing articles were distributed amongst the poor of the congregation of St. Paul's Wesleyan Church.

DEATH OF A FORMER RONC-ORN TRADESMAN.

'The death occurred last week of Mr. James M'Quinn at the age of 80 years, at his native place, Portwiliam, Stotland. For a great number of years he carried on business in Highsst., Rubcorn.and was connected with , the Wesleyan cause, being a zealous worker at Camden. " The Galloway Gaz,tte " referring to his death Oaten that he was of a gentlemanly, kindly, and courteous disposition, while at the same time possessing a vein of quiet humour that often sparkled out. He was an elder of the Free Church and took a warm interest in the Band of Hope connected .with the church. Prior to that he was superintendent of the Parish Church Similay School., He was also connected with other Christian work.

FIRN 11 A BEDROOM_ Al' RUNCORN.

On Wednes,lay night about 11.30 a tire waa discovered in a bedroom at the Sailors' Borne, Regent-st.. occupied by Mrs. Marsh. The fire brigade were appr'zsd of the matter, and they turned out speedily with the horse reel cart and the hand reel, in charge of deputy amerinten- dent Wright. They found that the bed in the room Occupied by Mrs. Marsh and her sister was on fire, but the police who were early on the spot had thrown the burning articles through the window into the yard below, and thus prevented the fire from spreading. The services of the firemen were consequently not required. The police who rendered first assistance were Constables Booth and Wylie. The damage is estimated at about £5, and is not covered by insurance. The origin of the fire is attributed to the falling of a lighted match. CAMDEN BAND OF HOPE MEETING. •

BRUNSWICK-ST. MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY.

In their annual report the secretaries congratulated themselves on it being the best one they had submitted. The class has at the pre- sent time a goodly number of young members, and they felt that their term of office had riot been without results. The president's decision to lead the claSs tor`another year had renewed the vigour of the mem- bers and had dispersed the gloom which was hanging over the annual meeting. The class had became an in- stitution of the St. Paul's and Brunswick centre of Methodism in Runcorn,and they hoped it would continue to be so. The secretaries in rclingnishing office said it was not that they were anxious to shirk work, but because they were desirous to bring some of the young men to the class into still closer intimacy with it. RUNCORN YOUTHS SHIPWRECKED.

EXCITING EXPERIENCES.

Throu I: the kindly aid of the Shipwrecked Mariners' Society, a Runcorn youth named John Watson, oi 85, Seaw-street, Runcorn, reached home on Monday after having suffered severe privations through being shipwrecked. lie was one of the crew of the schooner Stirling of Poway, a vessel frequently trading to Runcorn' and on her last visit here, Watson shipped on board her. The following particulars have been gleaned respecting the ship- wreck. On Sunday Joseph Fern, master of the Hull smack Columbus reached that port with the crew, six in number, of the schooner Stirling, The men had been rescued by the smack under trying and dangerous cir- cumstances and had it not been for the timely appear- ance of the Columbes,the crewe would undoubtedly have met with a watery grave. The Stirling was sighted by the Columbus between nine and ten o'clock on tho morn- ing of the 8:h inst., about 20 miles off Mordney, near Heligoland at that time the vessel Was flying signals of distress and, upon the Columbus ccming near, the crew requested to be taken off. Although a heavy sea was running it was decided to launch the smack's boat which although she was in a damaged condition by reason of two holes being stone in her was done. The damage was temporarily repaired by the use of grease, and manned by James Canavan mate; and John Morgan third hand, proceeded to the sinking vessel. Five of the crew were taken off in the boat but the captain refused to leave the sinking vessel, say- ing, he would see the last of her. Half an-hour after- wards the schonner disappeared and those on board the smack thought that the captain would perish with her. He, however, managed to float clear of the sinking vessel by means of a small skiff on deck, and from this he was eventually picked up by the fishing smack, Tire Colum- bus, at the time of the rescue, was short of pmvisione so that, with the addition of six men the outlook was, far frem cheering. Seven days elapsed from the time of the rescue to reaching Hull, and for four days the crew and the rescued men were almost without food or water. The mate of the schooner has been wrecked five times alto- gether, and upon two occasions he was the only man saved. The crew of the schooner state that their vessel was passed by several Dutch fishing vessels and one steamer, which took no notice of ,their signals. Watson, the only Runcornian of the crew of the Stirlitig, speaks in the highest tering of praise of the crew of the fishing smack, wets I-Allayed with conspicuous leravery. The only article watsan saved frotn the wreck, besides the clothes in which he stood, was an amber brooch he had purchased abroad for his mother, and in order to get this he had to enter the forecaele and wade through water reaching up to his neck. Needless to -say this is highly prized. -- News also reached Runcorn on Monday morning that the schooner " Chase " of Runcorn, had been wrecked off the coast of France the previous Saturday, and that one of the crew had been drowned. The vessel was in charge of Captain Brown, of Runcorn, and the only other Englishman composing the crew was an able-bodied seaman named Harry Johnson, also of Runcern. John- son arrived at his native town early this week, and an " Examiner " reporter took the trouble to " look him up," and gleaned a few particulars respecting the ship- wreck. Oa the B.h October last, the schooner which is the property of Mr. Cooper' ef Runcern,left Runcorn docks with a cargo of salt from Great Yet mouth. The vessel reached this port in safety, and having discharged her cargo she sailed for Keadley, al small maritime place on the Humber: Here she shipped a cargo of shoddy, and , departed for Cork on December 2nd. Heavy seas were experienced, but all went well until Friday night last when a terrible gale prevailed. The vessel was help- lessly driven out of her course and became stranded on the banks of the river Somme, about three miles from Cayeaux Semaphore, on the coast of France, early on Saturday morning. Again aid again the schooner was dashed on the sand banks,and the high seas lashed over her with tremendous force and fury. One of the crew named William Mendternative of Hamburgevras washed overboard and drowned. The Captain displayed great bravery and did his utmost to save the vessel, but his effortc were in vain, and the schooner became a total wreck. The crew repaired to the riggings, and were filled with hope shortly afterwards when they saw a lifeboat coming to their rescue. But the tempest raged with such fury that it was not until nearly tax o'clock at night on Saturday that the lifeboat was able to afford relief to the four remaining men of the crew,who had suffered great privations. A crowd bad assemoled on the banks, and on landing the distressed seamen were treated with the greatest hospi- tality. On Tuesday, Johnson—a fine, strapping young fellow—did not worn much the worse for his hardships, and jocularly remarked that he "didn't think his time had Come yet." Hs seemed glad that he had obtained another berth, and will soon again be traversing the mighty deep.

Many Roncornians will remember the large sailing ship " Stitherlandshire " which was the first vessel to , bring grain up the Ship Canal and was discharged at Roncorn lay-by. During the ship's stay here until the 20th of July last, the captain, Mr. Peter Nichol, made many friends, and the following exciting incidents con- nected with the voyage of the Sutherlandshire to ! Calcutta, will be read with interest. They are extracts ! from a, letter just received by Mr. George Hewitt, of Percival's-lanO, from Captain Nichol. "We got away from Ea.stham the day after we left Runcorn and all went well until the 3rd Ostober. On that day wo lost a ' man overboard, and nearly lost a boat and vier] men in trying to rescue him, but they returned in four hours without the poor unfortunate fellow. We struck our main top mast the same day,and were about ten days getting another fitted up, oand everything in its place again. Oae fine day the main yard cams down by a run which gave all the hands a fright. You may judge of the shock when there were seven tons of iron with a fall of 50ft. You will see that ,it has not been all clover this voyage." The rumour ' which had gained currency in Runcorn that the Suther- landshire had been lost will be seen to be incorrect.
