FOREIGN IffISSIONARY MEETING AT RUNCORN.

There was a good congregation in the Wesleyan Chapel, Ilalton-read, an Tuesday evening when the annual foreign missionary meeting was held. The chair was cccupied by Mr. John Simpson, and amongst those present were W. George, Rev. Edward Strutt, or Liverpool, and for several years missionary in Ceylon, Messrs. Harrison (Liverpool), Edward Taylor, J. R. Frith, S. Pope and W. Graham. After devotional exercises the Rev. W. George in pre- senting the general report of the Wesleyan Foreign Missionary Eaciety for the past year,stated that the year had not been marked by Swift and sensational progress, hut he a steady advance in the foreign district, and a "holding on" where progress seemed almost impossible. Their work in Italy had prospered More in the year than 1 lin any previous one. In Germany, the future looked very bright, and there seemed to be for them as Method- ists. a strong hope of having a large place in German life if hey were faithful to the teaching they had received. He also referred to the society's missionary work in Africa, India.and China. Their missionaries had been preserved in China during the time of great disturbance that had been recently, and during which some other missionaries had to render up their lives for the Lord's Sake. Mr. Taylor presented the financial statement, and stated that the contribution from the Runcorn circuit I was £279 last year, of which £25 was collected at Halton- road church and school. The collections at the services were £2 15 t met,ting £8 104. 31., collected by Mrs. George £47a. 61., collected in the school £3 163. 31. ; and Christmas cffering £5 104. 11. The enll.ctions for this year had been as follows : services £3 17s. 21., col-• leered bv Mrs. George £4 9s. 61., Sunday echos' £2 us. 61. Be urged the Sunday school scholars .to adopt the collecting books used by the Brunswick-street scholars, and he thought they would be able to raise a lamer sum. The Chairman in his address, remarked that people said that charity began at home, and there was so much to do that we could not spare anything to send mission- aries out of our own country. But he thought that they generally found that those who were most in earnest in doing pond at horns were men in earnest in sending the gospel abroad, and that persons who objected to foreign missions were not those who did very much good at home. _ _ _ _ _ _ _

The Rev. Edward Strutt, lite missionary in Ceylon delivered an interesting address on missionary work in Ceylon. That island, he said, had been well called "The rustling paradise of the tropics." Speaking of the inhabitants he said the island was a kind of microcosm, where there was a sprinkling of nearly all the races in India and Europe. But it was chiefly with the Tamils that he lived and laboured with when in Ceylon. They were llindoos, and enterprising, industrious, aober,quick at learning, naturally religious, and an affectionate and grateful people as he had reason for knowing. But although they had these virtues they had dark and terrible vices in their character. Re proceeded to refer to the methods suggested by many persons, as to what missionaries should do. The true idea for missionaries was not to use one method to the exclusion of the rest. They had to combine all the methods and then there was still room for others. Wherever they could get a few people to hear them they preached. They also preached by means of the press. The following was 12 months work at their prin"ing establisementa in Ceylon: 203 different -corks were published,3B9,66s copies were put into circolation, and the total number of printed pages was 12,915,296. They had the Bible printed in diffewnt languages, also the Wesley hymn book, Christian Mies, and other re- ligious works. By means of their schools they had raised up an army of readers. But Christians were roa. the only people who made use of the press. There was litera- ture distributed by the infidels of the West an enemy was sowing tares amongst the wheat. The untscior.tare's also adopted methods of controversv. In conclusion he referred to the suceess of the missidnary: eff:art, a ear- nestly pleaded for continued support oUotelgfl miscions. The speaker's and chairman's services were ackilow- legcs3 by the Rev. W. George. Tbe collection at the meetiag realised £9 15s.

Tao WESLEYAN Cracurr.—The quarterly meetinz cf ' the represt-ntatives of the Frodsham Circuit was hal at Trinity Chapel, on Wednesday afternoon. The met- ing passed a cordial vote of thanks to Mr. Carter, who has acted in the capacity of senior circuit, steward dur- ing the past three years, and it was desired that he should be re-elected to the office, but as Mr. Carter refused to serve, Mr. John Norcross, of Kingsley, was unaoimously elected, and Mr. Peter Booth, of Frodsham. was elected to the office vacated by Mr. Norcross, the, unior circuit stewardship.

"MEMORY AGAINST MEMORY." Thomas Unsworth, butcher, Buttermarket-street, sued Henry Hartley, butcher, Priestley-street, at the Warrington County Court, on Tuesday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, for £6 6s. 2d., the balance owing for butcher's meat supplied by the plaintiff to the defendant Mr. C. H. Unswortk appeared on behalf of the plaintiff, and Mr. Arthur Browne for the defendant. The plaintiffs case was that he had supplied to the defendant, nine sheep and other butcher's meat which came to a value of £l9 IG3. 2d., and that he had received from the defendant a cow for £l3 10a.,which he had credited, leaving the balanc-i which was claimed. Ile denied that he had ever received payment for it. Cross- examined by Mr, Browne: Re did not remember buying a beast off the defendant at Frodsham, in October, 1894, and paying defendant £l7 for it, receiving is. back forluck. He also denied that the defendant paid him £6 in the spring of 1893. Ile admitted that he had never sent the account for the present claim until the spring of this year,although the account became due in 1892. It was not cus- tomary te give receipts.—Mr. Browne submitted that it was a case of oath against oath. Here there were two respectable butcbers, one of whom said that the amount wcis owing, and the other that he had paid it, and it was for his Honour to decide which party was speaking the truth. He reminded his Honour that the plaintiff's books were kept very loosely, and that he had no ledger account kept against the defendant. The defendant was then called,and gave evidence about the plaintiff buying a cow rff him at Frodsham for £l7, which the plaintiff denied in his cross-examination. At this ' point of the case, the plaintiff voluntary told the court that he now remembered all about it, and that it was quite true. The defendant also swore that in the spring of 1893, he had paid the .£6 odd after having credited for the Cow. His Honour said that it was a case of memory against memory, and in consequence of the loose way in which the. accounts had been kept, he was inclined to think that the defendant's statement was the correct one,. inasmuch as he seemed to remember every transac- tion,and more especially he thought that the plain- tiff's recollection was had because in the first place he denied in reply to Mr. Brown's cross-examination all knowledge about the transaction at Frocisham, I and then he admitted that it was correct. It was evident that his memory was at fault, and judg- ment would be given for the defendant.

WARRINGTON.

RETURN OF TROOPS FROM ABROAD.

On Sunday afternoon, a number of soldieM arrived in Warrington from Halifax, Nova Scotia. The men, who were all in fairly gond health, had been abroad for a great number of years,and arrived' in Liverpool on Sunday morning after having hada rough passage. The troops who belotigPd to the Bth King's Liverpool Regiment, the Berkshire Regi- ment, and the Ordnance Store Corps, were to have. been landed at the Riverside Station, but owing to the heavy seas which were running at the time,they disembarked in the Alexandra Docks. SHIP CANAL.

LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

During the seven days ended Friday. December 13th, 111 seagoing vessels, with an algregate net registered tonnage of 35,504 tong, arrived at and sailed from the various docks, wharves, &c.,on the Ship Canal,as against 115 vessels of 33,691 registered tons in the preceding. week. The coastwise traffic was again exceptionally large, being represented by 87 vessels of 20.118 tons, against 90 vessels of 18,403 tons, the foreign trade was somewhat below the average, 24 vessels of 15,386 tons having usEd the canal, as compared with 25 vessels of 15,488 tons in the previous seven days. Amongst the cargoes imperted were thirteen of general merchandise, eight of china stone and clay, four of pig iron, two of timber and wood pulp, two of green fruitome of cotton and grain from America ,and one each of setts, boulders, macadam, limestone, slate, rock salt,.alt cake,copperas, tinplate bars,and steA billets. Twenty-five general car- goes were exported (including two to Bombay and one to Brazil), four of coal, and ore of salt.

From the " Morning " (London) Thursday, December 12th.

VACCINATION OF POOR CHILDREN.

ACTION BY THE CAMBERWELL GUARDIANS.

"Last night, at a meeting of the Camberwell Board of Guardians, a long discussion took place with reference to an item in the Infirmary Committee's report respect- ing the vaccination af a child eight days old, the daughter of an inmate of the Constance Road Work- house, who stated that, in consequence of the vaccina- tion, the child had suffered from eczema. "The Medical Officer reported that in his opinion the disease W 49 not due to vaccination. " Miss Evans moved 'That it be an instruction to the Medical Officer that in future no child in the worknouse be vaccinated under the age of six, if the mother object to the operation.' She said it had been proved beyond doubt that vaccination was a source of veat danger to life, and that it was respon,ible lot the spread of disease. No doubt the child in question had contracted eczt ma from anather child. As for the Medical Officer's state- ment, it was well known that doctors never put down anything as being due to vaccination. "Mr. Gordon seconded the motion. He thought it scandalous that persons should be compelled to have their children vaccinated at such an early age hecauee they happened to be poor, and, in consequence, were in the workhollAe.

" Mr. Woodman supported the motion. and said it was beyond dispute that two-thirds of the population of the country contracted swine kiPd of disease from vaccina- tion. It was bad enough to ho forced to vaccinate chit- dren.without making it necessary for the foul operation to he done at so termer an age. _ . . . . .

" The Clerk pointed out that the Medical Officer of a workhouse WhS bound, by the orders of the Local Government Board, to have an inmate vaccinated if he thought it necessary, and in the present case the Officer had thought it necessary. Therefore,if the Board passed the resolution, they sore placing the officer in an awk- ward preeicament. The motion practically overruled the Board's regulations. _ _ . . . .

"After further discussion,the motion was adopted by 10 votes to 2."

The above clipping, accompanied by a strong letter, was sent to our office by a local medical man. The note proceeds thus : " 'Taur excellent medicine, V. V. V., was never more urgently required than at present. Hav- ing thoroughly tested it in my practice I am fully alive to its value in the prevention and cure of the diseases ot vaccination. Many of the Profession are with you at heart, but dare not allow their names to be used. Time and the spl.ndid re.ults of your medicine will yet make V. V. V. a regular prescription. I regret I cannot allow you to publish my name." [We make no comment on the above,as we have never seen the need and the remedy so clearly explained before. —The V V V. THE WIGAN MURDER.

EXECUTION OF WINSTANLEY.

Elijah Winstanley, aged 31, collier, Wigan, was exe- cuted on Tuesday morning at Walton Gaol, Liverpool, for the murder of Railway Detective Kids, at Wigan. No representatives of the press were admitted, and the only intimations the crowd outside the gaol had that tne execution had been carried out were the hoisting of the black flag and the tolling of the bell at eight o'clock. Among those present at the execution were the Governor and Deputy Governor of the prison, Dr. Hammond and Dr. Beamiii, medical officers, Mr. H. L. Wright, under sheriff, and the Rev. D. Morris. the chaplain, who spent some time in the condemned cell on Tuesday nod administered Holy Communion to the prisoner. The chaplain stated that Winstanlev had behaved with great fortitude and resignation,but without the slightest trace of bravado,beinc, eviden,ly sincerely sorry for the crime he had committed. Billington was the executioner. The drop was 7 feet, and death was instantaneous. Since his conviction Winstanley had occupied one of the usual condemned cells,and from the first he had been resigned te his fate. During the visits of his family and relatives his chief anxiety was to learn what was likely to be the ultimate fate of Kearsley,who. he had all along a.rerted, was not directly concerned in the murder of Kidd. When, therefore,the announcement of the reprieve of Keanley was made kmawn to him he expressed the gratitude he felt, and looked as if a lead had been lifted from his mind. Soon after he relapsed into a state of apathy, in which he remained to the end.

At the inquest on Winstanley's body evidence wag given by the prison Governor and the doctors that the execution was carried nut in a skilful manner, death being instantaneous. The usual verdict was returned that the sentence had been properly carried out.

ANCIENT AND MODERN METHODS OF PRODUCING IRON.

At the last meeting of the 57th Session of the Liver- pool Polytechnic Society. 1104 in the Royal Institution on Monday lath inst., Mr. Thomas Morris, P.G S., of War- rington, delivered his retiring address as President of the Society. The subject chosen was "Ancient and modern methods of producing iron,' one which gave the lecturer ample scoDe of using to advantage his practical and theoretical knowledge, obtained by many rears ex- periences. Mr. Morris introduced his subject by quoting from Mr. Thomas Turner's work "The Metallurgy of Iron," and referred to a specimen of the metal in the Briripth Museum, which is believed to date from B.C. 3733, thus showing that iron was in use before the early

bronze period. Reference was made to the recent re- searches of Mr. A. Henry Rhine], and his exploration of the taamb of Selhan, which had not been opened for 2.000 years, and in which lustrous and pliant pieces of iron were discovered. Mr. Morris gave proofs which dispelled the idea of the non-existence of pure native iron; exemplifying the rich veins discovered in Siberia, supposed to be the result of some volcanic eruption. It was then pointed out that iron was made in this coun- try before the invasion of Caesar, the mines of Sussex bovine been worked before the Roman Conquest: an in- ' teresting description being given of the furnaces and methods used in those days. He next spoke ef the means resorted to by the savaee tribes of Asia and Africa for iron producing. Mr. Morris then described the "Catalan" process, and remarked on the similarity be- tween that, and the present Swedish method of iron making; dilating en the different kinds of fuel, and the expense attached thereto. An explanation was given of "direct prncesses " for iron manufacturing, and it was shown how the attempts in America had proved to be commercial failures. Flo teen spoke of Dudley, the first successful patentee connected with use of pitcoal, (patent granted 1621), hut Dudley was only able to pro- cluce'at first three, and subsequently seven tons of iron weekly. Mr. Morris described fully the present methods by which the various kinds of iron are produced, the descriptions tieing accompanied with many technicali- ties and details both novel and instructive.

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION. WARRINGTON v. BRIGHOUSE RANGERS.

These teams met on Sturdy on the Wilderspool encloaure before about 2,oool:spectators. The Rangers are about the most powerful combination in the whole of the clubs in the Northern Union, and thus far have the lk.ast number of point scored a gainstlthem. Further not a single Lancashire club has lowered their colours, and they, therefore, came with a splendid reputation, and had the weather been fine at the time -for kicking off, tkere would no doubt have been a much larger gate. When the teams came on the field, the Rangers looked veritable giants, and they had almost their best possible team. Mr. J. At Runcorn on Saturday. The ground was in a greasy state, making good play well nigh impo.sible. The placers faced each other as follows :—RuncorA : Full hack, J. Butterworth ; threequarter backs, T. WARRINGTON "A" v. WIGAN " A."—The return match between these teams was played at Wigan on Saturday before a mere handful of spectators. Owing to heavy rain the ground was a perfect puddle and good play was out of question. Wigan started and at once made tracts for Warrington lines but found a stumbling block in O'Brien who transferred play to half-way. Prescott with a long kick was the means of Wigan again pressing ancl Buckley being collared on the line, things began to Icok black for the visitors. Foden and O'Brien came away from the ensuing scrimmage and the Warrington for- wards reached halt-way with a good dribble. Scott , kicked well and tackling Seddon before he could return play rested in the home quarters. O'Brien dribbled over but only a minor resulted, and half-time arrived without any tangible score for either side. On the restart, Warringron pressed but could not score. Dixon and R rilton relieved and the Wigan forwards, adopting the rushing tactics, forced the visitors back ta their lines, Spruce averting the danger in grand style, for which he was loudly cheered. Warrington played with more dash on the restart and play settled at half way. W. Seddon kicked well and Mitchinson gaining possession passed to Dixon. and while the War- rington players were stopped, thinking that the hall was held, this player dropped a goal. _ RUNCORN v. LEIGH ( TEAMS) —At Leigh on Siturday in miserable weather. The game was of a forward na- ture, passing and back play being out of the question, which naturally favoured the home team, who were much heavier. Both teams were fairly representative, and a punctual start was effected. It was soon apparent that the homesters had the advantage in the parks, and their rushing tactics mitigated any attempt on the part of the Runcornians for open play, the game being one series of scrimmages. After a quarter of an hour's play, following some kicking bet Ween the respective backs, Jolley, the visitors' custodian, was caught nipping, and the homesters scored an easy try. This was followed by another one, a good individual effort on the part of Shovelton, who made a splendid run, and passed at the right moment to one of his threequarters, who got over at the corner. The interval arrived with the score,Leigh two tries, Runcorn nil. On resuming the Runcornians at once took up an attacking position, and the game be- came very vigorous, the referee having to caution the home team for their rough tactics. The games was a series of scrimmages and line outs, and was very unin- teresting. The final result was Leigh two tries,Runcorn nil.

WARRINGTON ST. MARY'S V. WIDNES " A " TEAM.— This was played at Widnes on Saturday, before a fair gate. Ihe Saints had to find substitutes for Beenett and Mason. STOCKTON HEATH.

COLLISION ON THE SHIP CANAL.—On Sunday morning as the steamer Fez, which was bound with a cargo Gf fruit from Valencia to Manchester, was passing up that portion of the Ship Canal running parallel with Elles- mere-road, Stockton Heath, she became for a moment unmanageable, not immediately answering her helm. The result was that she veered towards the right bank, along which she grated for several yards, and ultimately struck the corner of the stone buttress with her bows. A portion of the red sandstone was chipped off, whilst some of the bridge gear was damaged, preventing the complete closing of the bridge. This did not impede the traffic, however, as the bridge was brought together sufficiently to allow of carts and pedestrians to pass over. The steamer sustained some slight damage to her bows, but this did not delay her journey to Manchester.

THE WARRINGTON D RAM A TIC SOCIETY'S PERFORMANCES,

The members of the Warrington Dramatic Society gave three performances on Monday, Tues- day,and Wednesday last week in aid of the funds of the Infirmary. The Royal Court Tneatre had been generously lent by the proprietors to assist the society in its worthy object, and it is pleasing to state that public interest in the performances was also shown in a practical and substantial manner. Much long and patient labour is inseparable from the getting up of this class of entertainment,and to the officials of the society therefore a !need of . .

praise is firstly due for the arduous effort and self-sacrifice involved in organising the re- . hearse's, &c., and : secondly to all concerned for • the conSummatien of such effort in the excellent public performances of this week. On Monday ' -and Tuesday the farcical comedy in three acts by E. J.Lankester " Vhe Guy'nor " was played before numerous audiences which included many of • the elite of the town and neighbourhood,end it was .only natural that keen interees was shown in the performances as all those taking part in them were, with one exception, local amateurs. The comedy, - -which is based upon a tissue of misunderstandings -arising from the playing at cross purposes of nearly T. the whole of the characters, leads to many really 'funny situatione,and provoked much merriment in ; the audience. Contrary to pardonable anticipations • *the performances we? e singularly free from those 'innumerable mishaps and happenings of the un- foreseen which so often characterise amateur sepses tsentatims of stage plays. There was consequently a go" about the piece which was as agreeable to - the audierce as it was creditable to the pertormers "themselves. Nor was there happily, any need for the servic, a of the often obtrusive prompter, a fact which, in itself, speaks volumes for the painstaking 'work done in the rehearsals. Where all is so satis- factory it is therefore distasteful and unnecessary ' to discriminate on minor details in the Impersona- tions. Ths initial blundering of Mr. Butter- scotch is aceountal-le for most of the action, ' a large portion of which passes at his villa .at Fulham. And in this central part of "The Guy'nor " Mr. Alfred Woods was quite at home, and, having ample scope for his undoubted genius in low comedy pare, he made a great success of it. The somewhat similar par*, of "Old Maccle3field " was k happily borne by Mr. John E.Davies, an old favour- ite in amateur theatricals. The stolid stupidity with which the deaf old boatbuilder confuses a proposition for his daughter's hand with one for • /thelpurchase of a yacht bearing the same name, was ' a side-solitiing bit of acting. the whole piece is indeed, full of contretemps arising either from the jealouee of Butterscotch, or from old Macclesfield's • physical infirmity, though nearly all the characters contribute in turn to make confusion worse con- founded. As the stammering "Freddy," Mr. George It. Williamson had a most onerous part, and jug all the more to his credit therefore that in it he ac-' '- quitted himself in capital style. The character is by no means an easy one, a nice admixture of both manliness and boyishness being necessary to its due pourtrayal. In toe kindred part of "Theodore" Mr. S. Wellhead was not quite so good, although 'there was little to take exception to beyond the tendency to treat too lightly the occasional serious incidents which occur even in so farcical a piece, as, for instance, when upon revealing his solicitude for Kate, Aurelia mistaking him for the expected "Gregory," so unceremoniously sends him to the right-about. The fact that three members of Mr. Davies's household besides himself r had parts allotted to them in "The Guy'nor" t, is pretty conclusive that histrionic ability must "run in the family." Mr. Denis Davies' impersona- tion of the ungainly Yorkshireman, Gregory the groom, was true to the life, and the strong touches he was able to give to the character in voice, ges- ture,and manner, could hardly have been improved ,tipon after years of experience on the stage. Miss , 'Bessie Davies must be coupled with Miss K. Brund- , Tett as deserving of all praise in the parts of Carrie -and Katie The business of both was chiefly to sustain charming and unaffected girl-like characters, which ' natural advantages and an almost total freedom , from self-consciousness, enabled them to dc easily —and gracefully. The part of Butterscotch's maid " Ullage " was scarcely one in which Mrs. Davies :bad Any chance of shining or of doing justice to her acknowledged ability. On her appearance as " Mr. Macclesfield," Mrs. James Fairclough (the Mayoress) received quite an ovation, and the • thorough-going way in which she sustained the • part called forth repeated outbursts of applause. : Indeed,the whole ot!the scene at the boathouse,with the varied and often highly realistic incidents in - 'which Mrs. Macclesfield is to the fore,was certainly / not the least enjoyable of the three which make up the play. The dual characters of " jollicoe "and " The NlacTo Idy " were most ably pourtrayed by „ Mr. Charles H. Wood, albeit they are no slight test of bo,h ability and verger ility. The lifelike assump- tion of the Scotch character with its characteristic _ brogue, especially, was a pleasing aurprise to the audience. Miss Collas as " Aurelia " fully sus- tained the high reputation she has attained in War- rington in her association with the work of the Dramatic Society. At the conclusion of the second act there was a general and enthusiastic call before the curtain of the various members taking part in the play, and the opportunity was taken advantage of for presenting bouquets to the ladies. There was no falling off in regard to the success of the performances on Wednesday night, when "Caste"vvas toe play selected for representation. This Piece had been previously submitted to Warrington audiences by the Dramatic Society, but there was Certainly no flsgaing of interest on that account; in fact it wee evident that the large and apprecia- tive assembly expected the Society to repeat its past triumphs. In this respect the audience was certainly not disappointed. Speaking generally the acting all through the evening was of a high quality and the actors were frequently encouraged with rounds of applause. At the close of the second act all the performers were called before the curtain ana loudly cheered, the lady members of, the com- Dany being at the same time rewarded with beauti- ful bouquets of flowers. The characters were taken as follows: Bon. George D'Alroy. Mr. H. W. Cookson ; Captain Hautree, Mr. Gilbert T. Brun- drett ; Eccles, Mr. John E. Davies; Sam Ger- ridge, Mr. Alfred Woods; Dixon (servant to D'Alroy), Mr. Stanley Wright; the Marquise de St. Maur, Mrs. Kewley; Polly, Miss Collas; Esther, Mrs. John E. Davies. In the first part of the open- _ . .

_ • ing act, when D'Alroy tells of his love for the fair 1 t sther to his friend Hautree, Mr. Cookson 'thoroughly established himself with his audience. . Ris acting was confident and natural and his enun- ciation singularly clear and well-regulated, and he continued to display these qualities all through. The part of D'Alroy is a rather exacting one, and it gives plenty of opportunities for an unpractised , Player to strike the wrong note with awkward resulta. It is, however, nothing more than justice to say that Mr. Cookson escaped these pitfalls and Achieved a decided success. Mr. Brundrett was : perhaps scBrcel3 quite so successful as Mr. Cook- Son, There was occasionally a certain lack of f'•eedom in his playing and a want of emphasis in ‘., his speaking, but, even after allowing for these ex- cusable drawbacks, he gave what was on the whole an excellent interpretation of the character of the Aristocratic and kind-hearted Bautree. " Caste " 'without the bibulous Eccles would be something 1 ' 'like "hamlet " without the melancholy Prince and the Warrington Dramatic Society is singularly for- tunate in having amongst is members a gentle- tnan so capable of assuming this important role as Mr. J. E. Davies. When witnessing Mr. Davies's pourtrayal of Eccles'one can hardly help coming to .; two conclusions. One is that the character might • have been invented fot the display of Mr. Davies's i Special 'alents, and the other is that Mr. Davies is , a born actor—and we suppose that real actors, like ' real poets, are born, not made. It is needless to Say that the extravagances of the gin-loving Eccles were highly enjoyed by the audience. Sam Gerridge is another character which introduces the humorous element, and Mr. A. Woods ha before now shown his capabilities for doing justice to a part like this. The scene in which the practi- cal and irrepressible S. G. talks over with Polly their intended matrimonial relations was highly diverting, and was, as a matter of fact, decidedly well perform ed. The Marquise da St. Maur,a lady of ancient lineage and a strong upholder of caste, was represented with conspicuous success by Mrs. Rewley. It would be difficult for the most exacting critic to point out any flaws in this lady's acting. Alike in manner and tone she admirably delineated

the characteristics of the " 3farkissy," as Eccles with irreverent familiarity persisted in calling the high-born lady. Miss Collas, as the lively and impulsive "Poll" also contributed in a marked degree to the success of the performance. This character, as those who have seen " Caste " well know, is often on the stage and adds much to the sprightliness of the comedy, and it can be fairly said that Miss Collas was fully equal to her task. As the heroine, "Esther," Mrs. J. E. Davies won golden opinions. In the first act she perhaps dis- played a little stiffness, but this soon wore off, and in the second act in particular her acting was animated and pleasing. Mr. Stanley Wright had little to do as the servant of D'Alroy, but this little was done discreetly and well. In fact, we may in conclusion repeat that the whole performance was in the highest degree creditable to the performers and the Dramatic Society as a whole. Mr. T. J. Down added greatly to the enjoyment of the performances by being present with his large and competent orchestra, by whom, under his con- ductorship, the following items of music were ably discoursed :—Overture," Banditenstreiche" (Suppe); ballet music, "La Reine de Saba" (Gounod) ; polka," Merry Voices " (lierfert) ; Czardas, from "La Rosier° ' (Jakobowski) ; overture, " Light Cavalry" (Suppe) ; valse, " Amoretten Tanze " (Ounce!) ; march," British Patrol" (Asch). Furni- ture for the various scenes in all the performances was kindly lent by Mr. T. cutton, Legh-street, and the programmes were agreeably perfumed by Mr. J. Rymer Young. We understand that as a result of the three per- formances the Dramatic Society will probably be in a position to hand over the handsome sum of ..£lOO to the Infirmary Committee.'

WARRINGTON BANKRUPTCY COURT.

FRlDAY.—Before Mr. Registrar Ridgway.

, BANKRUPTCY OF A LOCAL IfORSE SLAUGHTERER. —Peter Robinson, 20, Church-street, knacker and horse slaughterer, also carrying on business at Arpley Meadows, appeared to undergo his public examination in bankruptcy. He was represented by Mr. W. J. Forshaw, and Mr. C. H. Unsworth also was present on behalf of a creditor.—ln reply to Mr. A. B. Patter, the assistant official receiver, the bankrupt said that the reason he filed his petition was that an execution was levied against him on November 21st. He had been a knacker or horse slaughterer for 40 years. He had had a very bad summer last summer, but the main cause of his in- solvency was an action for slander brought against h m by a man named Allen, a butcher of Earlestown, to recover damages for slander alleged to have been uttered against him in connection with a alead cow. The dam- awes claimed were £36s,and the damages recovered were £65. He was first aware of his insolvency immediately after the verdict of the action was given. His liabilities were £152, and assets £65 roughly, and the deficiency was fully accounted for by the costs and damages of the slander action. He had kept no books for his business because he could not write.He had lost a large amount of money on various racee,and on November 22nd, he put £lO upon a horse to win the November handicap,and £8 for a place. He gave the man with whom he bet the money in gold. He could not tell the name or address of the man. The bet was made at Lime-st., Liverpool, at the time he was going to the court in connection with the slander ac ion. Be drew £65 out of the bank on that morning. As he was proceeding with his examioa- tion, the bankrupt appeared to be confused, and at one time he said that the bet was made in Man- chester,and then in Liverpool. He fi, ally said that it was in Liverpool in Lime-st. On the first Thursday and Friday in November, he lost £4O ha bets. He had been drunk almost every night since the slander action. When he got home after the trial, he threw his bank book into the fire and knocked down his Bier, because the judge asked him if he was the prcper person to be entrusted with a license. Several receipts were produced, the dates of which the assistant official re- ceiver was of opinion had been altered, but the bankrupt denied that any alteration had been made as far as he was concerned. The reason that he destroyed his bank tr)ok was that he was drunk, and not as Mr. Potter suggested that it was to conceal the amount of money he had withdrawn from the bank on Nov. sth.—Cross- examined by Mr. Westwell, a creditor, he had been asked to apologise during the course of the slander action but he had refused to do so.-9y Mr. Forshaw : When asked to apologise by Mr. Collingwood Hope, he was asked to pay £5O and the costs. He was not asked to apologise betore the action commenced.—The hearing was adjourned until Jan. 24, 1896. Bertgavvrcy or e FARMER.—Luke Dumbill, farmer, Woolston Dam Farm, Wooleton, also underwent his pub- lic examination. He said that his failure was due to heavy rent, sickness, and losses owing to low prices. His rental was at the rate of £6 per acre, and he had re- sided on the same farm for 63 years. The ore micetion was closed.

INFECTIOUS CASES FROM RADGATE:

The CLERK read a letter from the Rev. J. J. Langbam with reference to the claim made by the Council for £l3 for the maintenance Of his son in the infectious diseases hospital while suffering from fever. In the dis- cassion that ensued it was said that a medical man from the borough was called in and he ordered the removal of the child to the hospital without the authority of the District Council or the knowledge of their Medical Officer.—Mr. KNOWLES pr vosed and Mr. HALTON seconded that the letter lie on the table.—The motion was carried unanimously.—The CLERK was deputed to visit the Town Clerk of Warrington on the matter.

===== 0002601_18951221_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
CLAIM FOR DAMAGES.

A letter was read from Mr. Jeans on behalf of Mr. M. Pownall, complaining that the Council had cut a• drain through a pasture field without giving him notice, and stating that he was entitled to damages for illegai entry and compensation for cutting the pasture.—Tha SUR- VEYOR said that due notice in writing had been given to the owner of the land.—Mr. KERFOOT said that no damage had been done as the turf had been re-laid. The ground was a trifle raised along the pipe line.—The CLERK said that if Mr. Pownall could prove damage the Board would have to pay.—The matter was left in the hands of the Clerk.

MEDICAL REPORT.

Dr. Sephton reported that the number of births was 24 and deaths 14. The death rate was equal to 12 per 1,000 against 14 the last three months as against 2.11 the corresponding month last year. The death rate from zymotic class equalled 1.4, per 1,000 against 1.8 the last three months as against 2.8 the corresponding month last year. There were two deaths fram the zymo- tic class, one from diphtheria and sue from measles. The following cases of infectious diseases had been reported : Scarlet fever 8 cases, erysipelas 2. The epidemic of scarlet fever at Winwick had entirely passed away. Measles were prevalent at Penkcth and it had beea de- cided to close the school there.

The Surveyor's and Inspector's reports were read and passed.

===== 0002601_18951221_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ALLIGATOR TRADE.

The attention of the United States Fish Commission has, says "Science Siftings," recently been called to a new and remarkable industry, that of the artificial pro- pagation of alligators for supplying the trade in Florida curiosities. The incubator system employed is quite simple. The eggs, which are about the size of those of the goose, are placed in boxes c.,f sand and covered up. The boxes are exposed on a roof to the torrid rays of a tropical sun, and in a few days the young reptiles are batched. It appears that a species of true crocodile le found in Southall Florida on both coasts. It is hardly distinguishable from the alligator,except by the shape of its head, though it grows bigger and attains a length of eighteen feet. Young crocodiles are batched for market in the same fashion. The mother crocodile lays her eggs in the sand like a turtle, and simply covers them over. Somewhat different is the practice of the maternal alli- gator, which in April or May seeks a sheltered spot on a , bank and there builds a small mound. • The foundation I of the mound is of mud and grass,and on this she lays some eggs. She covers the eggs with another stratum of grass and mud,upon which she:deposits some more e,e,ge Thus she proceeds until she has laid from 100 to 200 eggs. The eggs, in the course/of time, are hatched by the sun, assisted by the heat: which the decomposition of the vegetable material generates. As soon as they have "chipped the shell" the baby alligators are led to the water by the mother, who provides them with food which she disgorges, showing much anxiety for their safety. At this early period of their existence they are exposed to many dangers, being a favourite prey of fishes and turtles. Alligators' eggs, by the way, are sometimes eaten. Tile robbing of the nests for market is helping to hasten the inevitable destruction of the al- ligator fisnery, which has been an important and profit- able industry in Florida. The reptiles cannot long escape practical extermination. Already they are be- coming scarce, and the price of hides has gone up. It is estimated that 2,500,000 alligators were killed in Florida between 1880 and 1894. In 1890 about 250 I pounds of alligators' teeth were sold, hunters receiving from 4s. to Be. a pound f._r them. They are removed by 1 burying the heads and rotting out the teeth. Of the best teeth about seventy make a pound. The stuffing cf alligators and the polishing of the teeth give employ- ment to forty persons. Unfortunately, alligators grow .very slowly. At fifteen years of age they are only two feet Icng. A twelve-footer may be supposed reasonably to be 75 years old. It is believed that they grow as long as they live, and probably they live longer than any other creatures.

THE TREATMENT OF PRISONERS.

Sir,—Will you kindly allow me through the medium of your paper to draw attention to a common practice, which to me seems a great injustice and insult to prisoners under remand. I refer to the manner in which such persons are brought through our streets. They are compelled to walk from one of our railway stations to the police court in lrlam-et., accompanied by a gaol offi- cial, and in some cases (as on Saturday morning last) with both hands in the cuffs in front of the prisoner who had repeatedly to raise them to his head to pre- vent his hat from blowing off. It is England's boast that her subjects are considered innocent until proved guilty. and I fail to understand why those persons who may be innocent of the crimes alleged are treated in this manner, whilst guilty ones are provided with cab or other vehicle to take them through the street. Trusting that this matter will nut be allowed to drop until remedied.—Yours truly, Warrington, December. 1895.

JUSTICE.

COUNCILLOR A. BENNETT ON THE UNESI- PLOYED AND IRELAND.

Sir,—Having read carefully through the " report "of the proceedings at the Warrington Debating Society on Tuesday evening last, and failei to find the very im- portant pronouncement of the new Conservative mem- ber of our Town Council, on the subject of the unem- ployed and of Ireland, I thought it a pity that so much concentrated wisdom (?) should be lost, or kept frmi the free and enllghtened electors of Howley Ward ! Coun- cillor Bennett in pointing out the advantages that would accrue from Imperial federation, said that " our population was becoming denser and denser, our towns and cities were becoming crowded out, and the influx of people into thorn was augmenting the army of the ' unemployed, while in our colonies we had a practically inexhaustible outlet for our surplus population, who were only a nuisance to us !" If this had been a mere lapsus linguae, one could have forgiven him easily : but in his reply un the whole debate he was so impudent as to reiterate this more than indecent statement ; and,add- ing insult to injury, he threw the poor inmates of the workhouses into the epithet. This letter is the newest form of the Laisser faire principle of the "stupid party," as enunciated by the very latest form of politi- cal imbecility known as New Conservatism. I trust that the workingmen electors will remember this epithet of Mr. Bennett's when election time comes round.

Then as to Ireland, Mr. Bennett had sufficient know- ledge to know that Home Rule will have to be given to Ireland for the only objection he seems to have men- tioned was the name. " Don't call it Home Rude" says our new Conservative, " and you will have it in quick sticks." Ah ! but it was a glorious sight, to see this British patriot describing in glowing language the aver- age Englishman burning with a desire to hetp oppressed nations I (Armenia, Ilzr%) Has he ever heard tell of the penat law? Did be ever read of the massacres perpe- trated by the brutal English soldiers under Oliver Groin. well in Ireland ? Of the outraged Irishwomen and girls ? Of the Irish infants impaled on British bayonets ? Of the roasting alive of the poor fugitives in the Church where they had sought refuge? Oh, no ! Mr. Bennet: could talk glibly of the conquests (robberies) of England: but of Ireland's sufferings he knows nouuht. I trust that my fellow-countrymen will remember Mr. Councillor Bennett on the first of November.—Yours truly, Hon. Sec. I.N.L.

3. C. DILTON.

RE MESSRS. BISHOP AND CO.

Sir,—l repliad to a letter from the above firm in a former issue. Now, Mr.. Councillor Roberts appears to have taken on the cudgels and I must now repy to him. In the first place, it is most surprising that he could not say one word in favour of the wharf, which Messrs. Bi- shop in their original letter,said so important that several Councillors had been elected by the ratepayers, for their sake, but shirks the whole question. With respect to the wharf at Bank Quay now occupied by him, my conten- tion is that a party wall is usually a division between the property of two different owners, but in this case the respective owners built their walls about some 20 feet , apart without haying any opening upon the wharf,with the exception of one cottage near the railway ends and this wharf was used by the public from the time these premises were built until the Warrington Slate Company ' appeared on the scene. Mr. father wed it with9sit pay nient for many years down to 1861, the Vme-he vac.ited the adjoining premises, an I would advise the Coun- cillors above mentioned to have some stronger proof than Mr. Robert's assertion.

With respect to his question re stoppage of vehicular ' traffic by Litton's Mill, my reply is, that before the arpley Line was made, there stood upon the present line of railway a warehouse which was connec- ted with the present Mill by an overhead, as well as by a truck road upon the street, and the latter was so es- sential to their business that Messrs. Litton w-vld not allow even one of • their ivrn carts to cross from the stables to load at thd mill for fear of its beirvs damaged, and the Railway Company only bought Mr. Litton's land up to the present division wall. Mr. Thomas Lit- ton was very jeakus about the right of carts going over this road, and turned ali back which attempted to pass. With regard to the plan of extensions which the muni- cipal authorities refused to pass, the late Msgr.; Clerk. Mr. Brook, advsed us that he had not the slightest shadow of a doul+t that if we consented he would carry the plans through tho court successfully, but to avoid litigation we adopted other plans, and the town suffered considerably thereby in rateable value. To bis inquiry re Upper Mersey Commissioners, ,Mr. Councillor Roberts has once previously tried to turn me out and instal himself in my place, and unless he man- ages better next time I will follow the example of my predecessor, Mr. Alderman Davies, under the same circumstances—serve, as he served the time for which I was elected.

This letter will be my last reply on those subjects. I cannot pretend to correspond with Mr. Councillor Roberts, who only a fortnight ago showed his fondness of rushing into print by writing a letter to the local, Press, to inform the public that the Slate Co. did not keep mcney in their safe at niehts.—Yours, &n.. RICHARD T. FAIRCLOUGIL Mersey Mills, Warrington. --- AMBULANCE WORK AT WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Will you allow me space in your valuable paper to bring before ycur readers, and especially those who are generous and ever ready to help forward a good cause, what I consider an important subject that is, the ambulance work. lam sure if we could have a competition in our town so that the new numerous classes in and around Warrington might conanete, it would give a great amount of impetus to the movement which is now being so vigorously brought forward. There are, or have been, classes held at Padgate,Stretton, Penketh, Burtonwoori, Latchford, Stockton Heath, and in town at Bank Quay Station, Central Station, the Polict, I".S. A. Wycliffe, Y.M.C.A., R. A. Nayloes, Cros- field's \Soap Works, and in connection with the Ambul- ance Brigade. If a challenge cup or medals were offered I am sure each of the classes mentioned would get five of their best men to form a team, and enter for the trophy, and if such a competition is held (say in the Parr Hall) it would be worthy of patronage, and would I am sure encourage others to join in the noble and unselfish work of learning how to render" First Aid "to their fellow creatures in time of need, an:1860 cause those now carrying on the good work, to become if pos- sible more proficient. Trusting some more influential person will take this up, and thanking you in anticipa- tion, I beg to remain yours, &c.,

" FIRST AID."

THE EXAMINER SATFPDAY, DITENITIER 21 1'405.

RUNCORN FItEE LIBRARY LECTURES

THOUGHTS ON NATURE AND ART.

The third of a series of lectures under the auspices of ! the Runcorn Free Library was delivered in the Techni- cal Institute on Friday evening. The lecturer was Mr. John Skeaping, of Liverpool. and teacher at the Run- corn Technical Institute, and his subject was "Some Thoughts on Nature and Art." Mr. S. Pope presided. There was a good attendance. The Lecturer at the cutest, referred" to the excellent collection of books in the Library dealing with art, and urged his hearers to make good use of them. Proceed- ing, he said art influence was felt by all in a greater or lesser degree,hence their appreciative art public. Nat ere was the material of the artist, which, as a student he must learn to imitate exactly and minutely both in de- tail of form and coloureencl to so thoroughly understand the principles of light and shade that the design of his picture may be entirely arranged without any immedi- ate reference to nature ; objects and figures being after- wards accommodated to the designed arrangements,the light and colouring .beine possible but not necessarily t xisting. One often heard a picture eukeeised on the score of its being an exactly painted imitation of nature; ,imitated to the point of deception; and spoken of as the highest form of art. this was not only false—for such art was not merely in the lowest scale, but was coarse, savouring of the waxwork order in its motif, though infinitely superior to it in skill. The best photograpnic productions, with very few exceptions, left something to be desired, re say of the artist that he might take nature as he found her and produce a picture, was to say of the musician that he might thump on the piano and produce music. But just as the musician had to select kis -chords and arrange his harmonies to perfect his composition, so the artist in going to nature had likewise to order his com- position, selecting all that was necessary, rejecting much , that knight ma i tte picture, and frequently by redistri- buting the masses entirely, a tangled and disordered garden was reduced to a perfect paradise. Exact imita- tion might be found in a work of art, hut it was usually associated with study only. Who would deny that our grand cathedrals and beautiful dwellings of past centu- ries were works of art? Yet how much ef their beauty resulted net from similarity to nature, as from con form- Mg to the physical forces of internal vitality and gravi- tation. Thera was no mote common mistaken notion about the artist than that he was specially gifted—a I potion about which there was this savour of truth, that he had the imitative faculty in common with other in- telligent folk, but the extent of his success depended almost entirely upon the atnount of exercise he gave that faculty. What was called genius in the vast majority of cases resulted from the full exercise of all the working powers of the individual. The man of in- tutitive talent, whom they might call a genius from in- fancy was very rare. The true mission of art was to elevate the more; sense. To accomplish anything truly great it was necessary for artistes to think seriously for ! themselves and work sincerely and laboriously, not allowing their movements to be directed by traditional : methods of colouring and &silo which inevitably pro- duced a kind of mental paralysis. The soul, the foun- tain of all true art, being mutable, proeressive, and immortal threw off all limitation of the ancients and all traditional restrictions. A traditional art was unhealthy. On the study of nature most artists in all ages relied for the full development of mind, heart, and hand. On turning their attention to subjects to which their whole soul can go out they would achieve their greatest possibilities. Beauty of mind and true expression of the emotions were more important than beauty of form or skilful handling, though talent was necessary as the means for telling the story clearly and well. The lecturer proceeded to speak of a number of the most important pictures in the Liverpool Art gallery, criticis- ing many of them. Referring to the pre-Raphrelite school of painting he said it was impossible to deny that it had exercised a most beneficial influence upon the character of British art, much being due to such men as Millais and Hunt, but far more to the pen of Ruskin who took up the cudg4ls in their defence. The lecture was illustrated by a number of pictures shown on the screen by Mr. Clement Handley. A vote of thanks was passed to the lecturer and the lanterniat for their services, on the motion of Mr. Riley, seconded by Mr. Jones, and supported by the Chairman.

RUNCORN MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

On Monday evening, the Runcorn Musical Society gave their second concert of the season in the Public Hall, and we shall now have to wait until Lent for the concluding concert of their eighth season, when Mendelssohn's "Elijah " is to be given cn a most adt quate scale. The performance on Monday evening consisted of Harnett's " Ancient Mariner" and a miscellaneous programme, and from a musical point of view it was thoroughly successful and one in which a high standard of excellence was reached. The "Ancient Mariner "is 'work in. _which Coleridge's well-known poem has received an impres-ive touch at the hands ,f Barnett. The music was comnnsed expressly for the Birmingham Triennial Musical Festival, 1867, and has since then become a musical classic. It abounds in graceful and telling solo passages and is associated with most beauti- ful movements. The leading socalists were Miss Mary Langdon (soprano), Mrs. Burstall (contralto), Mr. William Green (tenor), and Mr. Arthur Barlow (bass), a !quartette who proved themselves equal to the exigencies of the occasion. It would be invidious here to speak of thl performances of each vocalist, bat special mention may be made of the singing of Mr. Arthur Barlow in the recitative and air, eeminencing" And now this spell was Kos pt," in which he most decidedly shone. In this, Mr. Barlow, was vociferously applauded and an encore demanded, but the lengthy programme forbade it. Of the concerted music, the impressive quar- tette " I fear thee, ancient Mariner the fascinat- ing duet " Fly Brother Fly." and the final quartette " What loud uproar bursts from that door," may be mentioned specially, but with regard to the latter, the honour must be shared by the chorus who were con- cerned in bringing it to a brilliant climax. There was , plenty of work for the chorus, which although supple- mented by a few Warringtnn voe.alists,woisl.l have been still better, if stronger. But in justice to them it must be stated that their singing was marked by that robust- ness of attack which characterised the previous perform- ance, and that their eines were appreciated was at- tested by the applause which the audience ttenerously and vieoreusly paid them. A nautical element eervades ' the choruses—in fact it runs throughout the whole pipes, —and may he said to be particularly appropriate for maritime town like R tricorn. The introductory chorus , was very effectively sung, and the same may be said of , the one commencing " About about, in reel and rout." The recitative by the male voices proved a very pleasing variation from the numerous recitatives allotted to the leading artistes. The orchestra had many important functions tc fulfil, and it must be admitted that the in- strumentalists ably coped with the demands which the performance of the " Ancient Mariner" calls for. Zr, the tuneful accompaniments to the onerous chorusert,the beautiful muted passages and sometimes strik- ing effects in the recitatives and airs, the Instrumentalists distinguished themselves. The second part of the programme was of a miscellaneous character, and contained solos for each of the principal vocalists, an overture for the orchestra, and Cowen's , bridal chorus from "The Rose Garden" for chorus and orchestra. "The Land of Yesterday" was sweetly sung by Mrs. Burstall, and she was followed by Mr. William Green who treated us in his fine tenor voice to an excel- lent rendering of " An Evening Sang "in which a subtle gradatien of tone was pleasingly conspicuous. Comply. jog with a recall he sang " The Anchor's Weighed " with such point and finish as to still further delight the audience. Miss Langdon was also successful in gaining the goodwill of the audience by her sweet rendering of " Nymphs and Shepherds" in which her soprano voice was heard to advantage. In response to the loud ap- plause, she twice bowed her acknowledgments, hut as it still continued, she complied with an encore Mr. Bar- low sang " lam a Roamer " (Mandelssohn) from the operetta" Song and Stranger," and it was well suited to his style. He gave it in such a true rollicking fashion as completely captivated the audience , who would not he denied an encore. Complying, he sang "The Jolly Blacksmith," in a skilful manner. In the overture " Poet and Peasant" (Suppi) the orchestra gave a most finished performance. The concluding, item —the bridal march—was a musical treat, and both chorus and orchestra seemed determined to make this piece the crowning gem of the evening. Mr.F.ll. Cross- ley wielded the baton with his accustomed ability, pilot- ing all through many difficulties ; and to him a meed of praise is due for the way he has conducted the society, and brought it to such a high state of excellence. Mr. R. Birkett Musgrove satisfactorily fulfilled tha duties of ac6nmpanist. There was ortlya moderate attendance at what proved a most int....resting concert.

On Saturday' night a man named Eurvill returned to his home at Folkestone in an intoxicated condition and threatened to shoot his wife. He loaded a fowling-piece and told his little daughter, three years of age, to go and fetch her mother. The child refused, and he dis- charged the gun at her, killing her almost instantly. The Duchess of York gave birth to a son at Sandring- ham on Saturday. The mournful recollections with which the 14rh of D'.cember has been connected in the minds of the Royal F unity will he dissipated to some extent by this auspicious event.

RUZiCO ri CO-OPER XTLVE SOCIETY.

TEA MEETING AT FRODSHAM.

On Wednesday evening, a tea party and entertain- ment was given under the auspices of the above named Society at the Town Hall, Frodsham. About 120epernons sat down to tea, the tables being presided over by Mes- dames Earlam, Youd, Yould, Harvey, Massey, Daven- port, Atherton,and Hulse. At the after proceedings the chair was occupied by Mr. E. Acton, of Runcarreand he was supported by Messrs. W.E. Dudley, D.Worthington, T. Riley, J. Sayle, and W. Upton (secretary). The Chair- man in the course of his remarks said he thought co- operation was the one thing needful for the present age. Great advancement had been made in every branch of science and art,and not least in co-operation, fie referred to the origin of the Runcorn Socieey in 1862, and stated that as soon as it was founded, opposition was set in array against it, but it had grown in spite of this,and it now had a business of over £45,000 per half year. They had 14 or 15 branches in Run- corn, Widnes, and the district. They desired that Frodsham should have a representative on the board of management, and the members would thus be able to see the workings of the society better. Tee entertain- ment was then proceeded with. The Runcorn Co- operative Glee Party contributed a glee; Mrs.Temperley a song; Mr. Withiugton, song; and Misses Davies and Withington a duet. Mr. Worthington delivered an address in which he referred to the objects of the society. He said co-operators thought they had taken up the good side of the socialistic movement, one side of wide i was perfect equality. All the members shared alike in the benefits. He would like to point out the disinterestedness of co-operation. He was sure that the Runcorn members would not gt.t an extra sixpence because they had opened a branch at Frodshem. Be gave an instance which had come under his own obeervation showing how the Co- operative Society had tended te make people thrifty. The capital of the society was now £45 53. 11d, and the major part of it was accumulated dividend and interest. With regard to the iatellectual objects, they must look at the gigantic society which a few workingmen had built up, and it had required some intelligence to bring it to its present state of PLIC.IO6g. Proceedi with the programme, Mr. 11. Johnson sang "Off to Pailadelphia ";' Miss Davies re citKi ; Mrs. Temperley again sang, land Messrs. Withineton and Cropper gave a duet. A recitation was &Ise given by Mr. T. Saunders ; and Mr. Wlthington was encored for his sane " I went with him." Mr. Sayle-proposed a vete of thanks to the Chairman and to Mr. Worthington for his address. The Secretary secoaded,and gave a spit it address on the inner workings of the society, and urged all present who were not members to join at once, and secure to themselves, the advantages, benefits, and privileges of membership.—Mr. Worthington and the Chairman suitably responded.—Mr. Dudley proposed a vote of thanks to the ladies, and to the artiates. Uc thought it had been clearly pointed out that co-opera- tion was the friend of the workingman.—Mr. T. Riley seconded. A successful evening terminated with the singing of the National Anthem.

G REENWAY-ROAD- BOMD SCHOOL.-

ANNUAL CONCERT AND PRIZE DISTRIBUTION. The Runcorn Public Hall was crowded with an appre- ciative audience on Wednesday evening on the occasion of the annual concert and distribution of prizes in con- nectiois with the Greenway-road Board Schools. The event has proved an usually good one in past years, and an Wednesday,the reputation was certainly not lowered. Mrs. Maitland Wood kindly distributed the prizes which were awarded for regular attendance and good work during the year. The proceeds of the concert are par- tially devoted to this object, and the remainder to the Orphan fund of the National Union of Teachers. The chair was occupied by Mr. S. Taylor J.P., C.C., and there were also on the platform Mr. James Pritchard (chairman of the School Board), Mr. F. J. Norman, J.P. and Mr. E, Imison, J.P, /tTlie Headmaster of the school (Mr. F. J. Moulton) informed those present during the distribution of prizes, that most of the boys receiving prizes had not missed once during the year. They were also being awarded for success in school work, for which examinations had been held in the various subjects. The Chairman then proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Wood for the graceful manner in which she had distributed the prizes. In do- ing so he stated that the evening's programme had been organised by the teachers, and would be carried through by the children at the Greenwav-road Board schools. It must be very pleasing to the audience ;to see so many children coming forward to receive their prizes. He thought it spoke well for education in Runcorn. The greatest credit was due to the head teachers and the whole staff,and he might say the parents also.(Applause) —Mr. Imison seconded the resolution, which was carried with acclamation and Mr. Imison respanded on behalf of Mrs. Wood.—Mr. J. R. Frith proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman aod to the donors of the prizes.—Mr. Moss (clerk to the School Board) seconded, and said he was sure they were all greatly obliged to the gentlemen who had responded to the appeals for prizes.—Mr. Prit- chard supported the proposition which was carried. The remainder of the evening was oocupied by the performance of the operetta entitled "The Pay of the Pied Piper." The selection proved to be particularly suitable, for it was just such a piece which enabled the scholars to display their abilities. The operetta is founded on Browning's well-known poem "The Pied Piper of Hamelin," and it brings out some very amusing situations. The Council are represented to be at their wit's end to ki.ow how to get rid of rats which infest their town. At length a wandering minstrel " The Pied Piper " appears befere them and undertakes to rid them of their vermin for 1,000 guilders. By the charm of his music he draws the rats after him to the river Weser, where they all drowned. The Council, seeing that they had nothing further to fear from the rats, refuse to pay him the fee agreed upon, whereupon by again playing his marvellous pipe he in- duces all the children of, the town to follow him. This has the desired effect upon the Council, and in the last scene the Councillors and officials appear to be only too glad to pay his fee and be rid of him. This was all ad- mirably set forth in the prologue which was intellioently delivered by one of the scholars named Arnold Senin". The operetta is one of three acts and the scenery for the occasion had been kindly lent by Mr. Caddick. Of the performance itself, one cannot speak ton highly, for each and all gave a most finished performance—the re- sult of continued training and painstaking care on the nart of the head teachers and assistants, viz.: Mr. Moulton (headmaster), Miss 11•Iburn (headmistress), Messrs. W. Kerman' J. C. Cragg, L W. Jeacock, Misses Acton, Simlcin, Ellison, Hill, and Mayall. The chief characters were represented as follows : The Pied Piper, Master S. Lane ; beadle, Master J. lianner ; the burgo- master of Harnelin,Master G. Jones ; the burgomaster's wife, Miss M. Haddock, and the following members cnnanesirig the Town Council: Miss Lily Hill, Masters W. Oliver, William Taylor, Leonard Ball, G. Andrews, and Joseph Ball. Most appropriate dresses were worn, and Mr. Ronald Jeacock is to be congratulated upon the manner he prepared the children for the stage. Master Lane very successfully fulfilled the duties of the piper, and his singing, especially of the song " A roaming life I lead," in his unusually sweet soprano voice was much appreciated by the audienee. Although Master Lane represented the piper, the piping which was sup- posed to have such a marvellous effect, was performed by Mr. Outhwaite in a most creditable manner. Master Banner was an ini- mitable beadle, and his amusing antics convulsed the audience to laughter. The dignified and honourable office ef bergomaster had a capable representative in Master G. Jones whilst of the couneillnrs, Miss Lily Hil had the most to say, and maintained her reputari.n of being a typical obstructionist, who meant to have her way. Several local and political " hits had been introduced into the piece, and greatly added to its inter- est. The lads who represented the rodents which were devastating the town were suitably attired, and their performance was the most amusing feature of the even- ing. The choruses without exceplino were well sung. An item calling for special mention Was the pretty danc- ing of about a score of girls, and the limelight effects in charge of Messrs. C. Handley and E. Lea, considerably improved this performance, which was deservedly en- cored. The duties of accompanist were satisfactorily fulfilled by Miss Kilburn.

EARL ESTOWN TONTINE SOCIETY. The fifteenth annual general meeting of the Pride of Earlestown Tontine Society was held at the Viaduct Hotel on. Saturday evening. Mr. A. Greatholder presided, and there was a large attend- ance of members. Mr. C. Aurilay submitted the balance sheet of last year's working, which showed the total receipts to have been £lO4 4s, 84. • On the expenditure side they had disbursed £54 7s. in sick and funeral money, and the total expenditure amounted to £75 13s. lA, leaving a balance dispos- able ot £2B Ile. 7d. This was divided by paying £25 3. 41. in dividend.and adding £3 Ss. 3•1. to the reserve,each member receiving Se. 101. The reserve fund now amounted to £53 84. 311. The balance sheet was adopted. Mr. Auclley was re-elected secretary, Mr. James Sharpe treasurer, and Mr. Greatholder, chairman. GOOD TEMPLARY.

MILD REN'Q NTERTAINMENT AT EA ALESTOWN.

Mrs. Brown, headmistress of the Earlestown district infant school, gave her annual entertainment in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Thursday evening. The room was crowded to excess,the audience frequently ap- plauding, the various items submitted. Mrs. Brown was assisted in the mana4ement of the children by Misses Attfield, Tripp, Banks, H,lsall, and Knowles, and judg• log from the character and excellence of the entertain- ment, a vast amount of time and trouble had been expended in order to bring the youngsters, who were all scholars in the infant department, to anything like efficiency. Mr. A. Peake, who has lately recovered from a srious illness, presided, and extended a hearty wel- come, asking the audience to bear with the little ones in any deficiency which might be apparent in the course of the entertainment. The children were all very prettily attired, the prevailing colour for the girls being white with a sprinkling of colour in the way of scarfs and sashes, and both they and the boys appeared to enter enthusiastically into the spirit of the whole thing, and by the manner in which they acquitted themselves showed at once that they had given all their attention to their tutors. Master Willie . Brown opened the proceedings with a speech, which he delivered very nicely, and in rapid succession the other items of a very long programme were proceeded with. These included action songs, drills, Kindergarten games, marching, recitations, musical drill, fan drill,&c. It would be almost invidious to single out any particular item for special mention, as without doubt all were excellently given. Perhaps, however, we should not be doing an injustice if we mentioned the actibn song " Lullaby and tip-toe song," in the centre of the platform being a cradle containing a little girl, another mite officiating as nurse, whilst other children were gathered round singing the lullaby. The action song "Fine old drum," by 40 children, the boys with small drums and the girls with tambourines, with a boy beating a big drum, was exceptionally well given. The dance by children of the first standard, was undoubtedly well executed, not a single hitch occurring. The recitations " Jack's letter " Harry Valentine, " A bad case " Lilian Lewis, " Waiting for the train" Lily Knowles. " An oversight " Mary Smith, "Dr. Longcoat and Mrs. Bonnett," M. Dunn, J. Laughton, were most amusing, more especially that entitled "An oversight." All the other items, as we have said, call for no comment, and Mrs. Brown and her assistants deserve every praise for the excellency of the entertainment produced. During the interval Mr. Peaks proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Brown and the whole of the teachers which was seconded by Mr. Borron. Miss Attfield presided at the piano dur- ing the evening.

===== 0002601_18951221_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLESTOWN PRIsIITIVE METHO- DISTS.

On Sunday special services wore held in the Primitive Methodist Chapel, Earlestown, in aid of the funds for the extinction of the church debt. The Rev. E.N. Shim- min occupied the pulpit at each service, and on each occasion delivered practical and interesting discourses. On Monday evening the Rev. F. N. Shimmin lectured to a fair audience in the Chapel on "Remarkable Charac- ters." Mr. E. J. George presided, and he was accom- panied on the platform by the Rev. E. R. Davies. The Lecturer said in the subject of his lecture they could classify the many departments of life, and select one department which would keep one talking for a week. But he would divide it into three heads, the true ruler, the true workrean,and the true woman. The true ruler. Be wanted them to consider the noble type of manhood presented in Tennyson's "King Arthur," a type of ruler holding power in a rude degenerate age. The type had all those qualities which had built up England into a first-class power. It would be a mistake for them to surrender their convictions to the view that our power lay in our armies. In this hour when we are passing through transitions, when it would seem that all about us is shaking from the centre to the circumference, we need men with clear heads, brave hearts,and into whose footprints we can put our own, feeling sure that our destiny as a nation shall not be wrecked. We want rulers, and he should like to touch upon an object lesson which touches upon the occasion. The massacres in Armenia : were a standing disgrace to the great Powers who had banded themoelves together to see that jus- tice shall be done, and a proof that when a weak man was at the head of the State, the mere puppet of his courtiers, the victim of his passions, swayed by his prejudices, we can see how much mis- chief can be done with such an unspeakable man called the sultan of I'itrkey. There was the proof of the value of a strong man, and we may well say with one of our leading papers, " Oh ! for One hour of Mr. Gladstone's leadership." We ask that those who govern shall govern in the spirit of this kingly man. Those placed in power should have character. The character was the secret of influence ;to do good we must be good. Were it not for character society would fall to pieces. It was the light of the world, the soul of the earth, the cement which holds nations together. Charactlr was the secret of resisting temptation. We admired such men as Washington and Garfield. He believed that Mr. Gla- dstone's power as a statesman lay in his spotless character, and from all sides, and all quarters people vied with each other in placift.: wreathis on his character. The True Workman. Ste would take them to George Elliott's "Adam Bede," who possessed a great amount of natural sagacity. He was thankful that George Elliott had drawn for them that splendid character ; he was a good son, a faithful brother and a strong manly friend. He then alluded to Mrs. Craig's book " John Halifax, gentleman," and said the lesson which that book taught was that a man might be in humble life and yet be a gentleman. There was much to be done to rectify the wretched iniquities which existed to-day. They ought to recognise the right of every man to rise. The gentle- man was the man who was master of himself. The true woman. It is said that there is nothing new under the sun. The new woman was the topic of the day. Where did she originate ? He found she was not so new as she was said to be. If he had read his Bible aright be found there were some new scorner, mentioned there. They say she is the imagination of journalists. If they looked at the new woman carefully, they would find she was one claiming perfec' equality, socially and politically, with man. Woman was made to be equal with man, his co-partner, to stand shoulder to shoulder with him. Woman's cause was man's to rise or sink together. Many a noble woman had helped a man through storms where his heart woaid have broken. There was no finer work on earth than the work of motherhood. Beheld the theory that all the front seats in heaven would be kept for the mothers, and he hoped the fathers would get a seat alongside. Let them feel that while at times they speak sneeringly of the new woman, they should not forget there were many maiden ladies doing noble work, who from principle remain single. To help the woman was to help the man ; the woman's cause was the man's cause. One mark of heathendom was that the woman was a mere beast of burden, the: victim of the lowest possible passions, but wherever Christ held sway the little child became the symbol of his kingdom. True women can shed a spiritual influence by the quiet intervention of commonsense goodly character, and so become what he called a " remarkable character." (Applause.) At the close, a vote of thanks was accorded the lecturer and chairman.

WE-:1TON.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.—This meeting was held on Friday evening, when the chair was occupied by the Rev. Canon Wood. There were also present the -Rev. D. S. Bowen, Messrs. T. T. Sill, J. Tudor, J. Ball, Savage,Vose, Guest, and J. Collier (clerk).—The Clerk read a letter from Mr. Ashton, saying the Rural District Council had resolved, subject to the sanction of the Local Govern • ment Board, that two places bo provided in the town- ship of Weston, one in the Weston Ward and the other in the Weaver Ward,for the deposit of nightsoil the former on land belonging to Mr. Thomas Ford, and the latter provided by Mr. John Jeffs, each at a rental of £5 to be paid quarterly, the tenancy to be subject to determina- tion by six months' notice.—Mr. Sill said the ques- tion had been satisfactorily settled.—The Clerk reported I that the Council had hitherto made a precept on the overseers for £35, and of this £2B 59. 11d, had been paid for election expenses. After the other items had been met there remained in hand 4s. 7d. In order to see them through to the end of the year he suggested that a precept for £5 be made upon the overseers," payable in January.—The Chairman said it would be interesting to the ratepayers to know the annual cost of the Council. —The Clerk said that to meet their expenses, when no elections occurred, the cost would be about £5 a year. Of course this did not include his salary, which came out of the poor rate, and which had to be increased by that amount. The total rateable value of the township he believed was £11,300, and a penny rate would realize about £4s.—The Chairman said the expenses of the Council would therefore mean one-ninth of a penny in the £, or with the salary included, one-third of a penny. Under these circumstances it could not be said that they were extravagant in their expenditure.—Mr. Sill thought it would ba wise to have a small balance in hand, to meet any extraordinary expenees that might arise.. He therefore proposed that A precept be issued on the over- seers for £lO, to meet the current expenses of the Vose seconded the motion, which was carried.
