NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

When the team representing the Brighouse Rangers came on the field on Saturday, there was much shaking of heads among the spectators as to the result of the match. The Rangers looked such giants by the side of the Warrington players that a win for the latter did not appear possible. These forebodings proved to be only too correct, for the Rangers inflicted the heaviest home defeat that Warrington has sustained during the present season.

Only the most sanguine of the Warrington supporters expected Warrington to win, for the Brighouse men came with a very good reputation, having never been defeated by a Lancashire club this season,and hearing by far the best defence of any club in the Northern Union. They have only suffered three defeats, and these were by Leeds, Bradford and Hull,places where they by some strange fatality are almost unvariably beaten. Previous to Saturday, their lines have only been crossod six times, so that despite their heavy defeat, Warrington, and Taylor in particular, have something to cotrgratu- late themselves noon, in being able to score at all. In no match have the Rangers' lines been crossed twice. Many at the commencement of the season predicted that Brighouse Rangers would finish at the top of the Northern Rugby League, and there is not a very remote probability that such will be the case.

On the day's play on Saturday, Warrington had decidedly the worst of it, though they pressed consider- ably, especially in the last half. They, however, lacked the ability to make the best of their opportunities. The forwards were much teo light to grapple with their opponents successfully, while the half backs did not play a great game by any means, though Clark showed up the best of the two. The three-quarters were given very little chance of saowing their capabilities except in the tackling line, but the bet back on the loiing side was undoubtedly lieesorn. He gave a splendid exhibition of full back play, and but for him, the score would have been much heavier. He was certainly the saviour of his side on many occasions.

To COM 9 to the players individually, Taylor was the best forward, and shone out especially in the line out. He also seized the opportunity to score which presented itself to ht,n, ad he is therefore one of the seven men who have I)wered the colours of the Brighouse Rangers. Of the o hI. forwards, Andrews, Barratt and Fairhurst wArn tl-p3 most prominent. Clark as was mentioned before, was the best hal.while ansonz the threequarters, Harbn was the most brilliant, while Cross was the safest. Burton dI I some good work, but his kicking was not so good as mutt. C trey, who took Broady's place, while not excepti ,nally brilliant, gave a good account of him• self, and proved a thorn in the side of the burly Anderton m,ny times.

WA3 undoubtedly Lswis Broo'te who won the match for the Racgers. Ele was a veritable terror to his oppon- ents, and when he once got going it was almost impossi- ble to hold him. Ile scored two of the tries, and the third wan entirely duel to his play. All three were wonderful pieces of work, especially the one which wee scored by the ball being passed from Brooke to Sugden.

The matches in connection with the Northern Union on Saturday were most peculiar. Four of them restited in pointless draws, while the remaining seven were all decisive victories with the exception ot the liunslet and Broughton Ringers game which was won by the form ir by two tries to one. Of the beaten teams only three, Warrington and Broughton Rangers and Liversedge scored, and the two former only scored a try each, and the latter a goal, while the victors scored an aggiegate of exactly 100 points. A hundred points to eleven is pretty good,and shows that the winning clubs scored an average of over 14 points each. When this is taken into consideration Warrington's defeat is not so bad after all.

The most decisive victory of the afternoon was that of Huddersfield, who beat Widnes bv a goal and five tries to nil. This shows that either Huddersfield is improving quickly,or Widnes is going a lot worse. It i most pro- bably the former, for it is only a fortnight since that Huddersfield were at the bottom of the taide, and now they have four beneath them.

Wakefield Trinity suffered—a —defeat almost as heavy as Widnes,for that was by 19 Points to nil, at the hands of Hull. Hull must be stronger than last year, for they have done by no means badly this year. Tyldesley was another club which had a decisive victory, for they belt Stockport by 16 points to nil. Liversedge could hardly have expected to he defeated by Manniugham on their own ground by 17 points to 5, but such was the case on Saturday.

Runcorn are still keeping a very creditable position in the League table, although in consequence of drawing with Leigh on Saturday they !drop from second place to third. They can still, however, afford to smile at their neighbourly rivals—Widnes and Warrington—who are about as near the bottom of the statistical ladder as they are to the top. There were very few Runcornians who were prepared for a draw on Saturday, but the unex- pected often happens and this proved to be the case. The men from the colliery town, who it will be remem- bered were decisively beaten on their native heath by the Runcorn lads, played with commendable dash and vigour' and were, if anything, superior to Runcorn in the forward ranks. The ground, tbo, was not in a desirable state for a good exposition of the game. The Runcorn team play an open ana combined game, aril in consequence of the greasy condition ef the ball it slipp^d from the players grasp in the passing movements, and thus prevented them on several from placing the result of the game beyond doubt. To-day (Saturday) Runcorn have a tough battle befcre them at Halifax. The Hali- fax men arc at present playing strongly, and gave evi- dence of it on Saturday when they severely thrashed Wigan.

Batley and Rochdale Hornets played a very lacks daiscial sort of a game, and they also failed to score. Wigan have not done so well of late, and on Saturday, they went down at Halifax by nine points to nil.

EXTENSIVE PROPERTY SALE A.T WAnnINGTON In consequence of the pending dissolution of partnership between Messrs. Samuel E. Johnson and Ralph Johnson, cabinet-makers, Warrington, Mr. Thomas Sutton, auctioneer, on Wednesday evening conducted a sale of their property, which is one of the largest and most valuable„of its kind in Warrington. The attendance included the Mayor of Warrington and a large number of well- known business men of the town. Mr. Samuel E. Johnson, who was confined to his home by illness, was represented by Mr. Spencer Well- head, solicitor. Mr. Ralph Johnson, besides being present in person, 'was represented by Messrs. Robert Davies, Kirconnell, and Co. The property was offered in four lots. Lot 1, which consisted of freehold business premises situate at the corner of Bridge-street and Rylands-street, containing show- rooms and offices, workrooms, storerooms, ¢zc.; lot 3, large three-storeyed cabinet works and yard situate in Ship-yard, off Bridge-street, leasehold and lot 4, four freehold cottages, were bought by Mr. Ralph Johnson at a total cost of £11,400. Lot 2, a freehold shop with land behind, situate in Bridge- street, !was /purchased by Mr. e. E. Johnson for £1,550. laws

ACCIDENT AT BIGFIELD VALVEHOIISE.—About a quarter to seven on Tuesday night, as Thomas Martin, waS climbing out of a trench at Bisfield Valvehouse where he had been ret airing a bursted pipe, tie slipped and fell a distance of 12 feet to the bottom, striking the pipe with his head as he fell, and receiving a severe gash. He wag conveyed in a cart to the surgery of Dr. Carruthers, where the wound was dressed, and thence to his lodgings at Bigfield, where he is at present. He is making good progress towards recovery and hopes to be well enough to proceed home to Liverpool in a few days. BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.-EXPLOSIONS FROM CO BESTIC BOILISIL:, The Water Committee dlr-ct the attention of their cus- tomers and ratepayers to the large number of explosions which have occurred during perlooe of severe frost from boilers used for ~omestic purposes. Most of 1 hese casualties might be prevented by the adoption of simple precautions either to prevent the freezing ot the water in the pipes, or to relieve coy excess of pressure which may arise in the event Of the water becoming frozen. She Water Engineer recommends the fixing of a reliable safety valve, and he will be pletsed to advise customers and ratepayers as to the size of the valve anil the proper position for fixing same. J. This was seconded by Mr. LONG, and carried unani- mously, Mr. Join; E. DAVIES suitably responding. Alderman Halms N, in moving a vote of thanks to Major Pickmere for presiding over the meeting,said that notwithstanding the unfortunate state of the Chairman's health he was present that morning to discharge his I duties. They all knew that he took a very warm inter- est in the success of the institution ; no one, in fact, could exceed him in that respect ; and they would all join with him in expressing a very fervent hope that next year would see him in completely rejuvenated. They hoped his life would long be spared to them and to his fellow-townsmen. (Hear, hear.) Mr. C. J. HOLMES seconded and said that those who had attended at the Bank during the last year could all testify to the courteous way in which Major Pickmere had conducted the business. The motion was heartily agreed to. Major PICEILERE replied, and the proceedings termin- ated.

TAKING COLD. A " Cold " is not necessarily the result of a high or low temperature. A person may go from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even, and not take cold. Be may remain nut in the calest atmosphere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contrary,he may take cold by pouring a couple of table- I spoonfuls of water upon some parts of his dress, or by standing in a door, or before a stove, or by siting near a window or other opening, where one part of tne body is colder than another part. Let it be irt,pt in mind that uniformity of temp r iture over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal heat upon am-rent parts of the body that produces Colds, by disturbing the uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn indunes congestion of some part. Keep the t eet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, kQep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food, and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its results. The very best Medicine, either for reventing or curing a Cold, is GWILYM EVAN'S QUININE BITTERS, THE VEGETABLE TONIC, Which is the most perfect Vegetable Remedy known,and the most efficacious to strengthen the constitution, en- liven the drooping or demoted spirits,brace the Nerves, and remove all Disorders of the Stomach which are caused by Indigestion, such as Loss of Appetite, distress alter Eating, Acidity of Stomach,Eructations. Heaviness and Langour. Disorders or the Liver are often caused tiv defective Digestion.
